5. Comparison

CloudTrail audits APIs; access logs record requests; Storage Lens aggregates storage metrics; Inventory lists objects.

S3 access control, sharing & access points

1. Concept

S3 uses policies and access controls to protect files and can share private objects within authorized permissions and time limits.

2. Purpose

Grant permissions to the correct bucket/object and share private files without opening all data publicly.

3. How it works

Permission check flow:

Client request
↓ IAM / bucket / access point policies
S3
↓ allowed
Object

IAM policy, bucket policy and ACL

  • IAM policy grants permissions to users/roles; bucket policy grants permissions from the resource side, useful for cross-account or AWS services.
  • Explicit Deny still beats Allow; SSE-KMS adds a key permission layer.
  • Bucket ARN is used for bucket level actions like ListBucket; object ARN is used for GetObject/PutObject. Do not confuse arn:aws:s3:::bucket with arn:aws:s3:::bucket/*.
S3 ACL — permissions on bucket or object
  • S3 ACL (Access Control List) grant basic permissions to AWS accounts or supported groups on buckets or individual objects; Does not have the same level of detail/conditions as IAM and bucket policies.
TypeScope and examples
Bucket ACLPermissions at the bucket level: READ allows listing objects; Do not allow yourself to read the content of every object.
Object ACLPermissions on each object: READ to read file; READ_ACP/WRITE_ACP to read/edit ACLs.
  • READ = list bucket or read object, depending on ACL type
  • WRITE = create/delete object at bucket level; Does not apply to Object ACLs
  • READ_ACP = read ACL
  • WRITE_ACP = edit ACL
  • FULL_CONTROL = full ACL permissions applicable to that resource
  • For example: Account A owns report.pdf and grant read permission to Account B via Object ACL when ACL is on.
AWS Account A
└── S3 Bucket
    ├── Bucket ACL → bucket grant permission
    └── report.pdf
        └── Object ACL → Account B: READ
  • ACL issued to account B through canonical user ID; B needs to allow his IAM principal to use the granted permissions. Explicit Deny and KMS permissions (if any) are still checked.
  • The new bucket defaults to using Object Ownership = Bucket ownership effective: ACL is disabled, permissions are managed by policy. Usually keep this default and use bucket/IAM policy for cross-account.
  • Block Public Access = guardrail prevents unwanted public opening; A public Allow is not necessarily effective force if guardrail blocks.

Pre-signed URL

  • The backend signs the URL for an action/key for a limited time.
  • The person with the URL uses signer rights for that request, without needing to receive an access key;
  • The URL is a bearer token so it must be kept private.
  • The URL does not override the signer's authority and may expire early when the temporary credential expires.
  • With SigV4 and IAM User credential, the maximum period is usually 7 days; Don't consider every URL to live 7 days.
  • Pre-signed URLs are not disposable by default. Upload browser needs proper CORS in addition to PutObject permission.

Access Points

  • Each Access Point has its own hostname and policy for the application/team, pointing to the same a bucket.
  • Bucket policy and access point policy must coordinate; Access Points are not bypassed bucket guardrail.
  • VPC-only Access Point limits access through the specified VPC, combining VPC Endpoint and endpoint policy.
  • Having a route to S3 still requires full IAM/bucket/access point/KMS permissions.
Multi-Region Access Point (MRAP)
  • MRAP provides a global endpoint for S3 buckets in many Regions. The request itself goes through the AWS network to the bucket with the lowest network latency among the available buckets active routing status; Integrated automatic MRAP AWS Global Accelerator.
  • Global users | v MRAP Global Endpoint | +-------+-------+ | | etc S3 Bucket A S3 Bucket B US Region Singapore | | +----- CRR -----+ Copy data
  • MRAP routes requests; CRR copies data. Cross-Region Replication must be configured separately between buckets; If receiving records in both Regions, two-way replication configuration is appropriate. CRR is asynchronous, so data does not appear immediately in the other bucket.
  • MRAP does not select a bucket based on whether the object exists or not: GET can return 404 If the selected bucket does not have an object. For example, US and Singapore users use the same endpoint to read replicated data in two Regions. ·

S3 Object Lambda

  • Object Lambda uses Lambda to change the content when passing through the Object Lambda Access Point, for example hiding PII or changing format; The original object is not modified.
  • From November 7, 2025, the feature is only available to existing customers and certain qualified partners.
  • Keep conceptual to understand old SAA documents; when the new design can be processed via Lambda/API Gateway/CloudFront or before saving. AWS availability change.

S3 ACL Details & Maximum Presigned URL Term

S3 decentralization mechanismScope & Limitations to remember
Bucket owner effective (Default)The new bucket completely disables ACLs by default; Only re-enable ACLs when you really need to authorize individual objects
Bucket ACL vs Object ACLBucket ACL control list/write permissions at bucket level; Object ACL Grants permission to read each individual object (no permission to edit object content via Object ACL)
S3 Presigned URL (SigV4)Creation via SDK/CLI has a maximum duration 7 days (or shorter if signed with STS temporary credentials about to expire)

4. Practical example

A customer receives an expiring presigned GET URL for a private invoice while the bucket stays private.

Download temporary private file: pre-signed GET. Browser uploads directly: pre-signed PUT. Data lake with multiple teams: Access Points and policy by prefix. Cross-account: Allows both the IAM side and the appropriate resource/key.

5. Comparison

  • A presigned URL temporarily shares the signer's permissions;
  • an Access Point has its own policy;
  • OAC secures CloudFront-to-S3 access.

Summary

  • Amazon S3: Stores files as objects in buckets. Useful for images, logs, backups, and large datasets; it is not an instance-attached disk.
  • Encryption, HTTPS & CORS: Encryption protects stored data; HTTPS protects transfers. CORS allows cross-origin browser access but does not replace S3 permissions.
  • Versioning, Replication & Object Lock: Versioning keeps earlier versions. Replication copies objects to another bucket. Object Lock protects versions against deletion or modification during retention.
  • Storage Classes & Lifecycle: Choose a class by access frequency and retrieval time. Lifecycle automates transitions or deletion; Intelligent-Tiering suits uncertain access patterns.
  • S3 Events: New or changed objects can trigger processing. Events can be delivered more than once, so applications need duplicate-safe handling.
  • Transfers & Batch Operations: Multipart uploads send large files in parts. Byte-range GET downloads selected ranges. Batch Operations applies tasks to many objects.
  • Logs & Storage Reports: CloudTrail identifies API activity. Access logs record requests. Storage Lens reports usage and storage trends; Inventory lists objects.
  • S3 Access Control: IAM and bucket policies control access. Presigned URLs temporarily share private files; Block Public Access helps prevent unintended public exposure.

CloudFront & Global Accelerator

Amazon CloudFront: CDN & origins

1. Concept

CloudFront caches content near users for faster delivery and less work at the origin.

2. Purpose

Deliver web/files via edge near users to reduce latency and backend load.

3. How it works

Cache at the edge

Viewer → edge location
          ├─ cache hit → return content
          └─ cache miss → origin → cache when policy allows → return content
  • Origin: The place holding the original content, such as S3 or a web server.
  • Edge location: A serving location near users. CloudFront returns a suitable cached copy immediately, or fetches it from the origin on a cache miss.
  • Distribution and cache behavior: The distribution holds delivery settings; a behavior selects the origin and caching rules for a URL path.
  • Content types: CloudFront delivers both static files and dynamic content over HTTP(S). Uncached requests can still benefit from optimized connections.
  • Origin location: An origin can be in AWS or on premises and need not share an AZ with CloudFront. VPC origins require supported Regions and resources.

Origins

Origin / mechanismFunctionFor example
S3 OriginGet content from S3 bucketHTML, CSS, JS, images
Custom OriginGet content from HTTP/HTTPS serverPublic ALB, EC2, web server on-premises
VPC OriginPrivate connections to VPC resources are supportedInternal ALB, NLB, EC2
Origin GroupPrimary + secondary origins group for failoverPrimary ALB fails → Secondary S3 serves the backup site
  • Origin Group is a backup group, not a type of server. Failover according to configured connection error/status codes, GET/HEAD/OPTIONS only; OPTIONS must also be enabled in cached HTTP methods. Do not use this mechanism to failover POST yourself.
  • VPC origin helps origin to be private, but must still meet SG/network requirements and supported features.
  • Public custom origins need to limit users from bypassing CloudFront if edge protection is required.

Cache Behaviors select origin

Path patternOriginRequest example
/images/*S3/images/logo.png
/api/*ALB/api/orders
Default (*)On-premises server/home
  • CloudFront reviews behaviors in the configured order and use the first matching behavior; Do not choose the most specific pattern yourself. If individual behavior does not match, use Default Cache Behavior (*).

Private S3 and OAC

  • OAC signs request from CloudFront, bucket policy for distribution is read; No need to open a public bucket.
  • Object SSE-KMS also needs a suitable KMS key policy.
  • S3 REST bucket origin is different from S3 website endpoint.
  • The website endpoint is a custom HTTP origin, not using OAC in the way of a private bucket origin.

TLS and edge protection

  • Custom domains need a certificate with the correct name.
  • ACM certificate for viewer → CloudFront must be in us-east-1; certificate for origin is a separate leg. AWS CloudFront certificate requirements.
  • WAF filters application requests; Shield helps protect against DDoS; signed URL/cookie controls viewer access to private content.
  • OAC grants CloudFront permission to read S3, without authenticating each user.

4. Practical example

European users retrieve images from a nearby edge instead of repeatedly accessing a Singapore bucket.

Global website/photo/video: CloudFront + S3. Private app needs edge delivery: VPC origin is suitable. HTTP(S) API: behavior/cache policy according to content and security requirements.

5. Comparison

CloudFront delivers content; S3 stores origin objects; Global Accelerator optimizes networking without caching.

CloudFront Video Streaming (VOD vs Live) & Custom Domain HTTPS

Streaming typeStandard architecture on AWSCache mechanism on CloudFront
Video On-Demand (VOD)S3 (Origin) → CloudFront → UsersDirectly cache static video files/segments stored on S3
Live StreamingCamera → Elemental MediaLive (encode) → MediaPackage (segments/playlist) → CloudFrontCloudFront does not encode but cache each created segment immediately (manifest keeps TTL short)
  • Custom Domain HTTPS on CloudFront: 1.
  • Apply for Public Certificate in ACM at Region us-east-1 2.
  • Declare Alternate Domain Name (CNAME) on CloudFront + attach ACM certificate 3.
  • Create Route 53 A/AAAA Alias pointing the domain name to CloudFront Distribution
CloudFront caching, delivery controls & geo restriction

1. Concept

CloudFront uses cache keys to select responses, TTLs to determine cache duration, and policies to control delivery.

2. Purpose

Cache the right content, update it at the right time and limit who can receive the content.

3. How it works

Cache stream:

Viewer requests
↓ Cache keys
CloudFront edge
├─ hit → cached response
└─ miss → origin → response / cache according to policy

Cache key

  • Cache key: The information CloudFront uses to decide whether two requests can share one cached response.
  • Components: Includes the path and can include query strings, headers, or cookies that change the response.
  • Example: Pages using ?lang=vi and ?lang=en need language in the cache key if they return different content.
  • Too many values: Each variation creates another cache entry, reducing reuse of cached content.
  • Missing values: Users may receive the wrong content. Private user data needs safe caching rules or no caching.

Cache policy and origin request policy

  • Cache policy determines key/TTL. Origin request policy transfers additional information to origin even though it is not in the key.
  • Forward header/cookie does not by itself guarantee a unique response.
  • Check cache behavior with origin's Cache-Control, especially minimum TTL and sensitive data.

TTL, file version and invalidation

  • TTL regulates cache time.
  • Origin changes the file without automatically making every edge get a new version immediately.
  • Use a version name like app.abc123.js for assets that don't change; HTML can have short TTL more.
  • Invalidation removes path from cache before TTL: /index.html, /images/* or /*.
  • Wildcard path helps delete many objects but invalidation still has quota/cost; It is not a mechanism to backup or repair the original file.

Signed URL and signed cookie

  • Signed URL matches a specific file; signed cookie merges multiple files such as videos many segments.
  • Both control the duration/conditions of private content access via CloudFront.
  • Block direct access to the origin if you want every user to pass this check.
  • S3 pre-signed URL is another mechanism, granting requests directly to S3 with signer permissions.

Geo restriction

  • Allowlist/blocklist by country in distribution based on Geo-IP.
  • VPN/proxy may affect location identification; do not change authentication/authorization by user.

Price Class

  • Price Class Choose a group of edge locations serving distribution to balance costs and latency.
Price ClassScopeTarget
100America, Canada, Europe, IsraelLimited to lower cost areas
200Class 100 + many regions in Asia, Africa, Middle East; There is VietnamBalance cost and coverage
AllAll CloudFront edge locationsLargest global coverage
  • For example, customers in Vietnam: Class 100 does not include edge in Vietnam, so you may have to go further; Class 200 includes Vietnam so latency can be reduced; All opens the entire edge network.
  • Actual costs depend on traffic and service area.
  • Price Class does not block users by location. People outside the range can still access it; If you want to restrict countries, use Geo Restriction/WAF.

Viewer Protocol Policy

PolicyMeaning
HTTP and HTTPSAllows both protocols
Redirect HTTP to HTTPSConvert HTTP viewer to HTTPS; HTTP/1.0 requests using write methods may be rejected
HTTPS OnlyOnly for HTTPS; HTTP returns 403
  • Policy applies to the leg viewer → CloudFront; The CloudFront → origin route has its own protocol policy.

Automatic Compression (Gzip/Brotli) & 4 Viewer/Origin Events hook points

  • Automatic Compression: when the browser sends Accept-Encoding: br, gzip, CloudFront automatically compresses eligible files using Brotli/Gzip at Edge, compressed and paid cache Content-Encoding.
Event HookRunning timeSupported by
Viewer Request / Viewer ResponseEvery request from the user to Edge and before returning (even when Cache HIT)CloudFront Functions & Lambda@Edge
Origin Request / Origin ResponseOnly runs when Cache MISS and Edge must call OriginOnly Lambda@Edge

4. Practical example

A website releases app.v2.js so long-lived caching can coexist with correct version delivery.

JS/CSS versioned: Long TTL. Quickly changed HTML: invalidation. Paid content per file: signed URL; multiple video segments: signed cookie. The content varies by query: the cache key contains the necessary query.

5. Comparison

A cache policy defines cache keys and TTLs; an origin request policy forwards additional information to origins.

AWS Global Accelerator

1. Concept

Global Accelerator accepts users through static IP addresses and routes connections over the AWS network to suitable application endpoints.

2. Purpose

Bring TCP/UDP traffic into the AWS network near the client, keep the IP stable and transfer it to a healthy endpoint.

3. How it works

Anycast and network line

  • Anycast = multiple network points broadcast the same IP;
  • The client goes to the appropriate edge and then traffic follow the AWS backbone to the endpoint.
Client → static Anycast IP → AWS edge → AWS backbone → regional endpoint
  • Global Accelerator IPv4 typically provides two static IPv4; dual-stack adds IPv6.
  • IP stays stable when changing backend, useful for firewall allowlist.

Endpoints and health

  • Endpoint group by Region contains ALB, NLB, EC2 or EIP according to the supported configuration.
  • Health controls where traffic is received; Endpoint weight and traffic dial adjustable distribution.
  • Traffic dial changes the rate of traffic entering a Region; endpoint weight divided in group. group.
  • When there is an error, the service changes network routing without having to wait for the client to run out of DNS cache get a new IP.

No cache

  • Global Accelerator accelerates the network, does not cache HTTP objects.
  • New content is still coming to the app; TLS, authentication and request control remain needed at the endpoint/application.
  • Do not confuse the Global Accelerator's static IP with the AZ static IP of an NLB: Accelerator sends global traffic to regional endpoints.

Target Endpoint types supported by Global Accelerator

  • Global Accelerator routes to all Internet-facing ALB/NLB, Internal ALB/NLB, EC2 Instances and Elastic IP in one or more Regions without exposing the internal network structure.

4. Practical example

A multi-Region UDP game uses stable IP addresses and routes players to appropriate healthy endpoints.

UDP game, VoIP, global TCP app; Multi-Region APIs need stable IP allowlists and quick transitions from faulty endpoints. Website needs to cache images/videos near the user: consider CloudFront.

5. Comparison

Global Accelerator optimizes TCP/UDP; CloudFront delivers HTTP(S) through a CDN with content caching.

CloudFront vs Global Accelerator vs S3 CRR

1. Concept

CloudFront delivers content, Global Accelerator improves connection paths, and S3 CRR copies data to another Region.

2. Purpose

Distinguish between content acceleration, network acceleration and persistent data replication.

3. How it works

Three mechanisms:

  • CloudFront = HTTP(S) delivery + cache
  • Global Accelerator = TCP/UDP acceleration, no cache
  • S3 CRR = copy object to another Region

Cache is different from replica

  • CloudFront cache may evict or run out of TTL; origin is still the data source.
  • CRR creates a real object in the destination bucket, requires Versioning and permission; The app that wants to read the target version must access the correct bucket/endpoint.
  • Do not use CloudFront cache instead of backup/DR for S3.
  • CRR does not automatically distribute HTTP to the edge or automatically move all clients to a backup bucket.

CloudFront is different from Accelerator

  • CloudFront understands HTTP and has cache behavior, signed URL/cookie, geo restriction.
  • Global Accelerator handles TCP/UDP and static IP routes; no key cache/TTL like CDN.
  • HTTP app can also use Accelerator when a fixed IP/network failover is needed.
  • Choose according to the main requirement, not just based on the word “global”.

4. Practical example

A website uses CloudFront for fast image delivery and CRR for a backup copy in another Region.

Photo website: CloudFront. Multi-Region UDP Game: Global Accelerator. Object needs another Region copy: CRR. It's possible to have CRR for DR and CloudFront for delivery, but you still have to configure origin/failover accordingly.

5. Comparison

  • An edge cache entry can be evicted;
  • a replica in a destination bucket is stored data governed by replication settings.

Comparison table

MechanismWhat to do?When choosing
CloudFrontCDN for HTTP(S), caches at the edge and passes requests to the originWeb/API, photos, videos, signed content
Global AcceleratorTCP/UDP over AWS backbone with static Anycast IP, no cacheGlobal app, IP allowlist, network failover
S3 CRRReplicate object to bucket in another Region, asynchronouslyDR, compliance, data must have a regional copy

Summary

  • Amazon CloudFront: Caches content at nearby edge locations for faster delivery and fewer origin requests.
  • CloudFront Caching: A cache key determines which requests share cached content. TTL controls how long it remains cached; overly detailed keys can reduce cache efficiency.
  • Global Accelerator: Uses stable IPs and the AWS global network to reach healthy endpoints. It does not cache content.
  • CloudFront, Accelerator & S3 CRR: CloudFront speeds content delivery. Global Accelerator improves connection paths. S3 CRR keeps object copies in another Region.

AWS Storage Extras

AWS Snow Family

1. Concept

Snow Family uses physical devices for data transfer or local processing where connectivity is limited, subject to product availability.

2. Purpose

Understand how to transfer data using a physical device when transferring over the network takes too long or the workplace lacks connectivity.

3. How it works

Why use equipment?

  • Hundreds of TB via slow transmission can take weeks/months.
  • Snowball Edge copies data on-premises and then transports the device to AWS for import into S3.
Create job → receive device → copy data → send back to AWS → import to S3
  • Compare network transmission time with receive/copy/send/import time; Actual bandwidth, cost and deadline must also be considered.

Snowball Edge and edge compute

  • Storage Optimized prioritizes capacity; Compute Optimized prioritizes processing at the place of data creation.
  • Edge compute integrates data filtering, inference or media processing at low-Internet sites before sending data to the cloud.
  • Data on the device is encrypted;
  • Key management, job credentials and shipping are still important parts of the process.

Import and archive

  • Import into S3 and then use Lifecycle to switch to the appropriate Glacier layer, do not choose the "Snowball import directly into Glacier vault" route.
  • Do not use the device for continuous synchronization that requires frequent updates; online recurring copy is more suitable for DataSync.

Note current availability

  • From November 7, 2025, Snowball Edge is only available to existing customers.
  • The Snowcone/Snowmobile in the old document is not the new option that is always available.
  • Keep the concept of offline migration for learning, but when implementing, check the service/solution currently provided. AWS Snowball availability.

4. Practical example

A site with a slow WAN needs to transfer a large dataset after verifying device availability for its account.

Data center has a lot of data and limited WAN: offline bulk transfer if eligible. Site disconnected needs to be handled locally: edge compute. The NAS has a good enough connection and needs scheduled copying: DataSync.

5. Comparison

Snow supports eligible offline transfers; DataSync transfers over networks; Direct Connect provides connectivity.

Amazon FSx managed file systems

1. Concept

FSx provides AWS-managed filesystems with Windows, Lustre, ONTAP, and OpenZFS options.

2. Purpose

Choose a managed file system with protocols and features suitable for the existing application.

3. How it works

  • Types of file systems: FSx for Windows File Server · FSx for Lustre · FSx for NetApp ONTAP · FSx for OpenZFS

FSx menu

TypeProtocol/strengthsSuitable
Windows File ServerSMB, NTFS, Active DirectoryWindows share, home directory, app Windows
LustreParallel file system, S3 integrationHPC, ML training, media processing
NetApp ONTAPNFS/SMB/iSCSI, ONTAP featureNAS/NetApp migration, multi-protocol
OpenZFSNFS, snapshot/clone/compression ZFSLinux/ZFS workload needs low latency

Windows File Server

  • Integrated AD, file permissions/ACL and user quotas.
  • Linux clients can also mount SMB; This is not an EC2 Windows-only service.
  • Multi-AZ deployment improves availability; Backup protects data.
  • On-prem access via VPN/DX with appropriate route, SG and file permissions.
  • Windows SMB shares often choose Windows FSx instead of EFS NFS.

Luster and S3

  • FSx for Lustre: Lets many machines read and process files at high speed, useful for scientific computing or model training.
  • S3 integration: Reads an S3 dataset as files and exports results back to S3.
  • Scratch: For temporary processing; it does not replicate data to protect against file server failure as Persistent does.
  • Persistent: For longer workloads and data that must be retained. Choose a deployment for its durability needs; FSx options have different failure protection.

ONTAP and OpenZFS

  • ONTAP is suitable for applications that rely on NetApp, multiple protocols, snapshots/clones and tiering.
  • OpenZFS is suitable for ZFS/NFS applications and corresponding snapshot/clone features.
  • Quick Clone does not replace independent backup.
  • Encrypt at rest using KMS; encryption in transit and file permissions depend on protocol/filesystem.

Integrate S3, Single-AZ/Multi-AZ & Storage tier of 4 FSx series

FSx seriesSingle-AZ / Multi-AZStorage optionsHow to integrate S3
FSx for Windows File ServerBothSSD / HDDCopy data over AWS DataSync
FSx for LustreSingle-AZ (file server)SSD / HDD / Intelligent-TieringOriginal link Data Repository Association (DRA) Automatically import metadata & export HPC results to S3
FSx for NetApp ONTAPBothSSD + Capacity Pool (Auto Tiering)Mount S3 Access Points (read/write via S3 API while data remains on FSx)
FSx for OpenZFSBothSSD / Intelligent-TieringMount S3 Access Points or switch to DataSync
  • FSx for Lustre Intelligent-Tiering Automatically transfer data through 3 layers: Frequent Access (< 30 days) → Infrequent Access (≥ 30 days) → Archive Instant Access (≥ 90 days); This mode's data has multi-AZ persistence but components Luster file server still runs Single-AZ.

4. Practical example

A company moves Windows file shares to FSx for Windows while retaining Active Directory sign-in.

Company Windows share + AD: FSx Windows. HPC reads dataset S3: Luster. Lift-and-shift NetApp NAS: ONTAP. Workload is using ZFS: OpenZFS. Shared Linux files across multiple AZs: compare EFS first.

5. Comparison

FSx Windows supports SMB/AD; Lustre targets HPC; ONTAP/OpenZFS serve their respective filesystem requirements; EFS uses NFS.

AWS Storage Gateway

1. Concept

Storage Gateway connects on-premises applications to AWS storage through familiar file, volume, or tape interfaces.

2. Purpose

For on-prem applications to use the familiar file/block/tape protocol with storage in AWS.

3. How it works

Gateway and local cache

  • Gateway: An appliance or software at your site that connects existing applications to AWS storage.
  • Local cache: Keeps frequently used data near the application for faster reads; data moves to AWS according to the selected gateway type.
  • Example: An office keeps using a familiar network drive while the data is stored in AWS.
  • Comparison: Storage Gateway provides ongoing access between on-premises systems and the cloud; DataSync runs transfer tasks and Snowball uses shipped devices.

S3 File Gateway

App on-prem --NFS/SMB--> File Gateway + cache --HTTPS--> S3 objects
  • The app sees the file share, S3 saves the object.
  • Gateway has IAM role to access bucket; SMBs can use AD.
  • Share/file permissions and S3 permissions are classes that need to be configured.
  • Do not consider the local cache as a permanent place for all data or that the gateway file is a shared POSIX filesystem like EFS.

Volume Gateway

  • Provide block volumes via iSCSI.
  • Cached volumes keep the main data in AWS, caching the portion in use locally to reduce local storage.
  • Stored volumes keep all core data on-premises and backed up to AWS.
  • Backup uses EBS snapshots to serve restore.
  • Stored is suitable when you need to access the entire dataset with local latency; cached is suitable when you want to reduce on-prem capacity.

Tape Gateway

  • Provides iSCSI virtual tape library (VTL) for existing backup software.
  • Tape stored on AWS, can be archived to Glacier Flexible Retrieval/Deep Archive according to configuration.
  • No more buying and shipping physical tape; Restoring tape archives still has retrieval time/costs to consider.

4. Practical example

Legacy backup software writes to Tape Gateway's virtual tape library without changing its tape workflow.

NFS/SMB share backed by S3: File Gateway. Block storage on-prem with cloud backup: Volume Gateway. Replace the tape library but keep the backup software: Tape Gateway.

5. Comparison

A gateway provides ongoing hybrid access; DataSync copies data in tasks; Transfer Family accepts files from clients.

Compare Volume Gateway: Cached Volumes vs Stored Volumes

CriteriaCached VolumesStored Volumes
Where full original data is keptAbove Amazon S3 (managed by Storage Gateway)Right now On-premises to access the entire dataset locally
On-premises disk space requiredShow Cache (recently read data) + Upload BufferEntire volume capacity + Upload Buffer
Backups on AWSVolume + EBS Snapshots scheduledBackup asynchronously to S3 as EBS Snapshots
AWS DataSync & Transfer Family

1. Concept

DataSync moves data between storage systems; Transfer Family lets partners transfer files using protocols such as SFTP.

2. Purpose

Distinguish between copying data between the storage system and the endpoint receiving files from users/partners.

3. How it works

DataSync

  • DataSync runs the task of transferring data between supported storage such as NFS/SMB/HDFS/object storage and S3/EFS/FSx.
  • There is scheduling, data checking, bandwidth limit and transmission over TLS.
NAS on-prem → DataSync agent/task → S3 / EFS / FSx
  • On-prem file storage often requires an agent; AWS-to-AWS in the same regular account no agent needed.
  • Some cloud/cross-account sources have different rules by location and task mode.

Metadata and sync

  • It is possible to keep permissions/metadata supported in source/destination pairs.
  • Copying NFS to S3 does not turn S3 into a POSIX filesystem with full file permissions as the source.
  • This is a transfer task that runs on schedule or on demand, not CDC database or real-time filesystem replication. AWS metadata behavior.

Transfer Family

  • Transfer Family: Provides an AWS-managed file transfer server; partners use familiar SFTP, FTPS, or FTP clients to send files to S3/EFS where the protocol is supported.
  • AS2: A separate option for business-to-business data exchange, using supported storage destinations.
  • Login and permissions: Depending on the protocol, use service-managed accounts, AD, or a custom identity provider. IAM and filesystem permissions limit each user's file access.
  • FTP: Unencrypted and restricted to VPC endpoints; unsuitable for sensitive files over the Internet.
Partners using SFTP client → Transfer Family → S3 prefix are authorized

Select by task

RequestSelect
Copy large NAS to AWS, with schedule/verificationDataSync
Partners still use SFTP client to send filesTransfer Family
On-premises apps continue to use hybrid file/block/tapeStorage Gateway
Just need a private network lineVPN/Direct Connect; Also need transfer tool

4. Practical example

Partners upload files over SFTP to S3 through Transfer Family; internal NFS data moves with DataSync.

Migration NFS → EFS: DataSync. Scheduled file synchronization: DataSync. Instead of self-managed SFTP server, data goes to S3: Transfer Family.

5. Comparison

DataSync suits storage migration and synchronization; Transfer Family suits partner-facing file-transfer protocols.

Transfer Family: SFTP Server vs Connector & Identity Providers

ComponentThe active party opens the connectionData stream
Transfer Family Server (SFTP Server)External partner clients connect to your EndpointClient actively Uploads or Downloads files from S3 / EFS
Transfer Family Connector (SFTP Connector)AWS is proactive Connect to the partner's external SFTP ServerExecute command SEND (S3 → partner) or RETRIEVE (partner → S3)
Identity Provider on Transfer FamilyUser authentication mechanism
Service-managed usersTransfer Family stores username + directly SSH Public Key and private S3/EFS directory mapping
AWS Managed Microsoft AD / AD ConnectorSign in with a business Active Directory account
Custom IdP (Lambda / API Gateway)Integrates external identity store, supports custom password + SSH key
AWS storage selection guide

1. Concept

Choose storage for how the application reads and writes: disks, shared files, objects, or transfers from existing systems.

2. Purpose

Choose storage based on how the application accesses data first, then consider durability, latency and cost.

3. How it works

Select according to how the application reads/writes:

Block → EBS
Shared File → EFS / FSx
Object → S3
Local temporary disk → Instance Store

Block, file or object?

DemandService
Object/API, data lake, static assets, backupS3
Disk for EC2, boot or databaseEBS
NFS/POSIX files are shared by many Linux clientsEFS
SMB/AD or specialized file systemFSx
Local disk is very fast, data can be recreatedInstance Store
  • Do not choose S3 as disk mount for databases that need block I/O.
  • Don't choose EBS so that multiple apps across AZs can share files.
  • Archive object: select the S3 Glacier class according to the required retrieval time.

Where is the data located and can it be lost?

  • EBS is in one AZ, if you want to go to another AZ, you need a snapshot/restore.
  • Regional EFS across multiple AZs; One Zone reduces fees but accepts AZ risk.
  • Instance Store loses data over the instance/host life cycle.
  • FSx has different deployments; Check HA/backup of specific type.
  • Backup prevents accidental deletion; replication/failover serves availability, not replacement replace each other.

Hybrid access or migration?

TaskService
Continue file/block/tape at on-prem, AWS backendStorage Gateway
Online bulk copy/sync has verificationDataSync
Receive SFTP/FTPS files from partnersTransfer Family
Offline bulk transferSnowball/physical transfer solutions are also available

4. Practical example

An application needing shared Linux files chooses EFS; an analytics team stores object-based lake data in S3.

Windows shared drive: FSx Windows. WordPress multiple EC2: EFS. Separate DB Disk: EBS io2/gp3 by I/O. Data lake: S3. Before closing, check RPO/RTO, throughput, encryption and transfer/retrieval cost.

5. Comparison

Identify block, file, or object access first; then compare durability, performance, AZ scope, and cost.

Summary

  • AWS Snow Family: Moves data using physical devices when networking is too slow or unavailable. Check device eligibility and availability for the account and use case.
  • Amazon FSx: Provides specialized filesystems: Windows for SMB and AD, Lustre for high-performance processing, and ONTAP/OpenZFS for their respective features.
  • Storage Gateway: Lets on-premises applications use AWS storage through familiar file, disk, or tape interfaces.
  • DataSync & Transfer Family: DataSync copies data between storage systems. Transfer Family provides file transfer through protocols such as SFTP.
  • Storage Selection: Choose by access pattern: EBS for disks, EFS for shared files, S3 for objects, and FSx for specialized filesystems.

SQS, SNS, Kinesis & Amazon MQ

Amazon SQS

1. Concept

SQS queues work so the application submitting jobs and the application processing them need not run at the same speed.

2. Purpose

Use managed message queue to:

3. How it works

  • Decouple producers and consumers · Asynchronous processing · Buffer traffic spike · Retry workload when consumer fails

Basic flow:

Producer
↓ SendMessage
SQS Queue
↓ ReceiveMessage
Consumer
↓ processing
DeleteMessage

Why do we need SQS?

If the producer calls the consumer directly:

Producer
↓
Consumer
  • Consumer is slow/error → producer is also affected.

With SQS:

Producer
↓
SQS Queue
↓
Consumer
  • SQS keeps messages temporarily so: Producer scale independent · Consumer scale independent · Traffic spike is buffered

For example:

Web App
↓ 1,000 video jobs
SQS
↓
Workers handle it gradually

SQS = decouple + async + buffer.

Message Lifecycle

Producer
↓ SendMessage
SQS
↓
Consumer ReceiveMessage
↓
Process
↓
DeleteMessage
  • Producer: An application sends a job to SQS with SendMessage, such as an order confirmation email.
  • Consumer: An application receives the job with ReceiveMessage and processes it. Receiving a message does not delete it.
  • Successful processing: The consumer calls DeleteMessage to remove the job from the queue.
  • Failed processing: If it was not deleted, the message becomes visible after its visibility timeout so a consumer can retry.
Message
↓
Visibility Timeout is over
↓
Visible again
↓
Another consumer retry

Visibility Timeout

  • Visibility Timeout: The time SQS temporarily hides a message after a consumer receives it, so another consumer does not immediately take the same job.
  • Successful processing: The consumer must call DeleteMessage; expiration of the timeout does not delete the message.
  • Unfinished processing: The message becomes visible again when the timeout expires. Consumers must tolerate receiving the same job again.
  • Configuration: The default is 30 seconds and the maximum is 12 hours. Use ChangeMessageVisibility to adjust it during processing.
  • Choosing a duration: Too short can cause duplicate processing; too long delays retry after a consumer fails.
Message
↓ Receive
Temporarily invisible
↓
Consumer processing
DeleteMessage
↓
Messages disappeared
Visibility Timeout is over
↓
The message reappears
↓
Retry
Timeout is too short
→ duplicate processing

Timeout is too long
→ slow retry when consumer crashes

Visibility Timeout = time the message is hidden while being processed.

Dead-Letter Queue (DLQ)

If an error message persists:

Receive
↓ fail
Retry
↓ fail
Retry
↓
maxReceiveCount
↓
DLQ
  • DLQ is used to hold: Poison messages · Messages error processing multiple times
  • → help debug or reprocess later.
Main Queue
↓ fail many times
DLQ

SQS Standard Queue

  • SQS Standard: A queue for large volumes of work when strict ordering is not required.
  • At-least-once delivery: A message can be delivered again even if it was received before.
  • Best-effort ordering: SQS attempts ordering but does not guarantee every message arrives in send order.
  • Example: Before charging order 123, check whether the transaction was already processed so a repeated message does not charge twice.
Order ID = 123
↓ 1st processing
↓ duplicate
second processing

SQS FIFO Queue

  • FIFO is used when needed: Strict ordering + Deduplication
  • Queue name must: *.fifo

customer-A
↓
1 → 2 → 3

While:

customer-B
↓
1 → 2 → 3
  • Two different groups can process in parallel.
  • Multiple MessageGroupIds = increase parallelism

If there is only 1 group:

every message
↓

  • → lower throughput.

FIFO Deduplication

  • FIFO supports: MessageDeduplicationId; or: Content-Based Deduplication; to avoid sending the same message multiple times in the deduplication window.
  • FIFO deduplication ≠ business exactly-once processing
  • Consumers should still design idempotent.

SQS vs SNS

Points that are easy to confuse: SQS = Queue = a message processed by a consumer

SNS = Pub/Sub = one fan-out message to many subscribers

If multiple systems need the same event:

Producer
↓
SNS Topic
├─ SQS Queue A
├─ SQS Queue B
└─ SQS Queue C

→ Each system has its own queue.

Amazon SNS

1. Concept

SNS sends one notification to multiple subscribers, such as email and order-processing systems.

2. Purpose

Using pub/sub push format for a publisher to send the same event to many subscribers.

3. How it works

Publisher
↓ Publish
SNS Topic
├─ SQS
├─ Lambda
├─ Email / SMS
└─ HTTP(S)

SNS = Pub/Sub + Push + Fan-out.

Topic, Publisher & Subscriber

  • Producers just need to publish to: SNS Topic
  • SNS will push messages to appropriate subscriptions.
Publisher
↓
SNS Topic
↓
Subscribers
  • Popular subscribers: SQS · Lambda · Amazon Data Firehose · HTTP / HTTPS · Email · SMS · Mobile Push
  • → Publisher does not need to know how many consumers are behind it.

SNS Fan-out

A message can be sent to multiple systems:

Order Created
↓
SNS Topic
├─ Fraud Queue
├─ Shipping Queue
└─ Analytics Queue
  • Each subscriber receives a copy of event.
  • This is called: Fan-out

SNS + SQS

Pattern is very important:

Producer
↓
SNS Topic
├─ SQS Queue A
├─ SQS Queue B
└─ SQS Queue C
  • SNS: = fan-out event
  • SQS: = keep message = retry = buffer = consumer processes at its own pace

For example:

Order Created
↓
SNS
├─ Fraud SQS
├─ Payment SQS
└─ Shipping SQS
  • → Each system is independent, a slow consumer does not affect other consumers.

  • → No need to create multiple SNS topics just to separate message types.

Amazon Kinesis Data Streams

1. Concept

Kinesis Data Streams retains continuous data so multiple applications can read independently and replay it during retention.

2. Purpose

Collect and save real-time streaming data for multiple consumers to process independently.

3. How it works

Producers
↓
Kinesis Data Stream
↓
Shards
↓
Consumers
  • KDS is suitable when needed: Real-time streaming · High-volume events · Ordering · Multiple consumers · Replay data

Data flow

  • Producers can be: Application · IoT Device · Clickstream · Logs / Metrics · Kinesis Agent
  • Consumers are usually: Lambda · Application / KCL · Amazon Data Firehose · Apache Flink
Producer
↓
Partition Key
↓
Kinesis Stream
↓
Shard
↓
Consumer

Shard & Partition Key

  • Shard: A part of a Kinesis stream that receives and stores some of its records.
  • Partition Key: A value on each record that Kinesis uses to choose a shard, such as customer-123 for that customer's events.
  • Same Partition Key: With key-based routing, records go to the same shard. For send ordering, use sequential PutRecord calls; batched PutRecords does not guarantee ordering.
  • Ordering scope: Ordering is within a partition key in a shard, not one global order across the entire stream.
Record
↓
Partition Key
↓ hash
Shard
customer-123
↓
Shard A
customer-123
Event 1
Event 2
Event 3
↓
same shard
↓
keep the correct order

Hot Shard

If partition keys are unevenly distributed:

Many records
↓
same Partition Key
↓
with Shard
↓
Hot Shard
  • Bad example: Partition Key = "default"
  • → Almost all records are put into one shard.
  • You should choose a well-distributed key such as: Customer ID · Device ID · User ID

Retention & Replay

  • KDS does not delete records immediately after the consumer reads them.
Record
↓
Kinesis Stream
↓
kept in retention period

According to the slides:

24 hours
→
365 days

During that time:

Consumer A → read
Consumer B → read
Consumer C → replay
  • → multiple consumers can process the same data independently.
  • Other points of SQS: SQS = message is processed by consumer and then deleted
  • Kinesis = record still exists in retention = can be replayed

Capacity Modes

  • There are 2 modes: Provisioned · On-Demand
1. Provisioned
  • You manage the shard number.
  • Each shard follows the slide: Write = 1 MB/s or 1,000 records/s
  • Read = 2 MB/s

If traffic increases:

Traffic increased
↓
add shards
  • → suitable when the throughput is relatively predictable.
2. On-Demand
Does not self-manage shard numbers
↓
Kinesis self-scales capacity
  • → suitable for workloads that are difficult to predict or want to reduce capacity management.

Throughput/pricing details may vary by AWS; remember priority Provisioned = shard management, On-Demand = AWS scales itself.

Consumer Throughput

  • There are 2 main ways of reading.
1. Shared / Standard Consumer
Shard
↓
many consumers
↓
share read throughput
  • Multiple consumers reading at the same time will share the capacity of the shard.
2. Enhanced Fan-Out
Shard
├─ Consumer A → 2 MB/s
├─ Consumer B → 2 MB/s
└─ Consumer C → 2 MB/s
  • Each registered consumer has: 2 MB/s / shard / consumer
  • → suitable when many consumers need to read the stream with high throughput and low latency.

KPL & KCL

1. KPL
  • Kinesis Producer Library = supports producers in sending records effectively
2. KCL
  • Kinesis Client Library = supports consumers reading shards + coordinating multiple workers + checkpoint
  • Checkpoint Help consumers remember: Which record has been processed?; to continue processing after restart/failure.

Data Size & Storage

  • According to slide v48: Record max = 10 MiB
  • KDS is suitable for: Multiple streaming records · sent continuously

KDS only keeps data during the retention period:

  • Kinesis = temporary streaming storage ≠ long-term data lake

If you need to save long term:

Kinesis
↓
Firehose / Consumer
↓
S3

Security

1. In Transit

HTTPS / TLS

2. At Rest
  • KMS Encryption
  • Access control: IAM · Resource Policy
  • Can be used: VPC Endpoint; Traffic to Kinesis does not need to go through the public Internet.

Overall flow

Producers
↓
Partition Key
↓
Kinesis Data Stream
↓
Shards
↓
Lambda / KCL / Flink / Firehose
↓
Real-time Processing / Analytics / S3

One consumer analyzes website clickstream for fraud while another calculates real-time metrics.

5. Comparison

A stream retains events after reading for replay; queues such as SQS normally delete successfully processed messages.

Kinesis vs SQS

Mistaken points: SQS = Queue = async job processing = consumer processes then deletes

Kinesis = Stream = real-time data = many consumers read the same data = has retention + replay

Video processing job
↓
SQS
Clickstream / IoT / logs real-time
↓
Kinesis
Amazon Data Firehose

1. Concept

Data Firehose receives streaming data and delivers it to configured storage or analytics destinations.

2. Purpose

Bring streaming data to storage/analytics destination using managed service, not self-managing shard and consumer fleet.

3. How it works

Delivery pipeline

Producer / Kinesis Data Streams / logs
 → Firehose → buffer / optional transform → destination + backup S3 when configuring
  • Destination includes S3, Redshift, OpenSearch and HTTP/vendor supported. Delivery Redshift uses S3 as staging before COPY.

Buffer and latency

  • Buffer: Firehose collects data until a size or time threshold before delivering it, such as batching logs for S3.
  • Latency: Delivery is near real time but usually not instantaneous. It is not the default choice for reacting immediately to every event.
  • Zero buffering: Some destinations can reduce or skip buffering; Firehose still is not a stream where several consumers independently replay history.
  • Capacity: AWS manages delivery, but source and destination quotas and limits still apply.

Transform and format conversion

  • Lambda can transform records before delivery.
  • Format conversion native converts JSON to Parquet/ORC for S3, using Glue Data Catalog schema;
  • CSV needs to be transformed to JSON first, not directly converting all formats.
  • Compression/columnar format helps reduce storage and scan data when querying. AWS Firehose format conversion.

Replay, backup and permissions

  • Firehose does not provide retention streams for consumers to freely replay like Kinesis Data Streams does.
  • If multiple consumers/replays are needed, put KDS first and Firehose as a consumer delivery.
  • There are retry and S3 backup for all/failed records depending on destination/configuration.
  • Service role needs source, destination, Lambda and KMS permissions; Design error monitoring and reprocess backup data.

4. Practical example

Application logs pass through Firehose, convert to Parquet, and land in S3 for Athena queries.

Logs/clickstream → S3 data lake; data → Redshift/OpenSearch; Lambda transform → JSON → Parquet. Need to process real-time multiple consumers and read back events: Kinesis Data Streams.

5. Comparison

Firehose is a managed delivery pipeline; Data Streams provides a stream for custom consumers and replay.

Amazon MQ

1. Concept

Amazon MQ provides AWS-managed ActiveMQ or RabbitMQ brokers for applications using those messaging protocols.

2. Purpose

Use managed message broker for legacy applications that need traditional messaging protocols/APIs.

3. How it works

  • Amazon MQ = managed ActiveMQ / RabbitMQ = keep old protocol = lift-and-shift compatible

ActiveMQ & RabbitMQ

  • Amazon MQ supports: ActiveMQ · RabbitMQ
  • Clients still use: Native Library · Native Protocol
  • Broker can provide: Queue · ≈ same as SQS · Topic · ≈ same as SNS
  • but still retains the routing, acknowledgment and semantics of ActiveMQ/RabbitMQ.

High Availability

  • Amazon MQ can deploy multiple AZ brokers depending on engine/configuration.

For example:

  • AZ A · Active Broker · AZ B · Standby Broker

When active broker fails:

Active Broker fail
↓
Standby Broker
↓
Client reconnect
  • → increase High Availability.

According to the ActiveMQ architecture in the slide:

Active / Standby
↓
Shared EFS Storage

Network & Security

  • Broker runs in: VPC

Security Group controls:

Producer
Consumer
↓
broker connected or not
  • Commonly used authentication: Broker credentials · Native broker authorization
  • IAM is mainly used to manage: Create / Delete / Configure broker
  • You don't have to replace the broker credential for the data plane.
  • Encryption: In Transit = TLS
  • At Rest = KMS-backed encryption

Overall flow

Existing Producer
↓
AMQP / MQTT / STOMP / JMS...
↓
Amazon MQ Broker
↓
Queue / Topic
↓
Consumer

If the broker fails:

Active Broker
↓ fail
Standby Broker
↓
Client reconnect

4. Practical example

An application using JMS and ActiveMQ moves its broker to Amazon MQ to minimize code changes.

SQS/SNS uses AWS's own API.

But the old app might be using: AMQP · MQTT · STOMP · OpenWire · WSS · JMS

If rewriting to SQS/SNS is too expensive:

Existing Application
↓ keep current protocol
Amazon MQ

→ no need to change the entire messaging code.

Old Java / JMS / ActiveMQ app
↓
Amazon MQ
RabbitMQ app needs
exchange / binding / routing
↓
Amazon MQ
Hybrid app uses
AMQP / MQTT / STOMP
↓

5. Comparison

MQ preserves familiar brokers and protocols; SQS and SNS use AWS-native messaging models.

Amazon MQ vs SQS / SNS

Amazon MQ = traditional message broker = protocol compatibility = runs on broker instances

SQS / SNS = AWS-native messaging = serverless / managed scale is better

Legacy app needs AMQP/JMS/MQTT
↓
Amazon MQ
New AWS-native app
↓
SQS/SNS preferred

Amazon MQ does not scale nearly infinitely like SQS/SNS.

You still have to pay attention to: Broker size · Storage · Connections · Throughput · Queue depth

SQS vs SNS vs Kinesis vs Firehose vs MQ

1. Concept

SQS queues jobs, SNS broadcasts notifications, Kinesis retains streams, Firehose delivers data, and MQ provides compatible message brokers.

2. Purpose

Select messaging based on data reception, replay and application compatibility requirements.

3. How it works

Queue another stream

  • SQS workers often share message processing; delete message when finished.
  • Kinesis keeps records in the retention window even after a consumer reads, allowing multiple consumers to read independently or replay.
  • SQS Standard has at-least-once/best-effort order; FIFO uses message group/deduplication to support consistent ordering.
  • Kinesis orders by shard, not a global order across all shards.

SNS + SQS

An event → SNS ┬→ Queue A → order processing
               └→ Queue B → analytics
  • Each system has its own queue, retry and scale.
  • SNS push to queue; worker pulls from SQS.
  • SNS does not have to replace every queue;
  • archive/replay of SNS FIFO is a separate feature, it does not mean every SNS topic is streamed like KDS.

Kinesis + Firehose and MQ

  • KDS keeps the stream for analytics/replay; Firehose pushes a branch to S3/warehouse.
  • As long as managed delivery is needed, you can use Firehose directly without adding KDS.
  • MQ compatible migration app needs JMS/AMQP/MQTT/STOMP or semantics broker engine. engine.
  • Broker has finite capacity and needs to choose HA deployment; new app can be used SQS/SNS as appropriate.

Limit Payload, FIFO Deduplication & SNS → Lambda Resource Policy

ServiceMaximum sizeAnti-infection Mechanism & Authorization
DynamoDB Item400 KB (including attribute names and values; large file saved to S3 + holds S3 key in DynamoDB)Use Conditional Writes when idempotency is needed
SQS Message1 MiB (larger payload using S3 Extended Client)SQS FIFO filter accordingly MessageDeduplicationId or hash the content in the window 5 minutes
SNS Message256 KiB default (supports scaling up to 1 MiB / Extended Client with S3)SNS FIFO filter in 5 minutes just before fan-out to SQS FIFO subscribers; when SNS calls Lambda is required Lambda Resource Policy allow lambda:InvokeFunction with Principal: sns.amazonaws.com attached SourceArn

4. Practical example

Pending orders use SQS; events needed by multiple systems fan out through SNS to several queues.

Background jobs: SQS. One event to many systems: SNS + SQS. Telemetry multiple consumers/replay: KDS. Logs to data lake: Firehose. Legacy broker app needs few code changes: MQ.

5. Comparison

Choose by ordering, replay, fan-out, retention, and protocol compatibility rather than throughput alone.

Key comparison

ServiceModelDifferences
SQSQueue, workers pull/divide workBuffer job, retry/DLQ; message is deleted after processing
SNSPub/sub pushFan-out event to many subscribers; filter each subscription
Kinesis Data StreamsStream has retentionMany consumers read/replay; ordering by shard/partition key
Data FirehoseManaged deliveryBuffer/transform sends to destination, does not replay stream arbitrarily
Amazon MQManaged ActiveMQ/RabbitMQ brokerCompatible with existing protocols and broker applications

Summary

  • Amazon SQS: Queues work for consumers to process. Standard can redeliver messages; FIFO preserves per-group order and supports deduplication.
  • Amazon SNS: Sends one notification to multiple destinations. Combine it with SQS so each system has its own queue and processing pace.
  • Kinesis Data Streams: Retains events for independent readers and replay within retention. Reading does not remove an event as completed queue processing normally does.
  • Amazon Data Firehose: Receives streaming data and delivers it to storage or analytics destinations such as S3, without managing a delivery-consumer fleet.
  • Amazon MQ: Runs AWS-managed ActiveMQ or RabbitMQ brokers. Useful for existing applications that need their current messaging protocols.
  • Messaging Selection: SQS queues work; SNS broadcasts; Kinesis retains replayable events; Firehose delivers data; MQ serves applications using traditional brokers.

ECS, Fargate, ECR & EKS

Amazon ECS – Orchestration, tasks, and services

1. Concept

ECS manages containers according to task definitions and the number of copies you request.

2. Purpose

Deploy containers, maintain the required task count, and replace failed tasks without building orchestration.

3. How it works

  • Amazon ECS is AWS's container orchestration service. You declare the container you need to run, its quantity and resources; ECS takes care of deploying, monitoring and replacing tasks when they fail.
Container Image
↓
ECS
↓
EC2 or Fargate

3 main concepts

ECS conceptNature
Task DefinitionThe blueprint declares the container image, CPU/RAM, ports, environment variables and IAM Roles
TaskAn actual running instance is instantiated from the Task Definition
ServiceThe dispatcher maintains the correct quantity desiredCount Tasks are always running and attached to the Load Balancer

For example:

Service desiredCount = 3
↓
Task 1
Task 2
Task 3
  • A Task dies → ECS creates a new Task to replace it.

ECS Cluster

  • Cluster is Logical group contains Service, Task and compute capacity.
ECS Cluster
├── Service A
│   ├── Task
│   └── Task
└── Service B
    └── Task

Cluster is not a server.

Container flow

ECR Image
↓
Task Definition
↓
ECS Service / Run Task
↓
EC2 or Fargate
↓
Containers run
  • Service Used for apps to run continuously.
  • Run Task Used for jobs that run once.

IAM Roles

These two roles are easy to confuse:

Task Execution Role
= ECS is used to start Task
→ pull image from ECR
→ send CloudWatch Logs
→ get Secret
Task Role
= code inside the usage container
→ read S3
→ call DynamoDB
→ send SQS
  • If you run ECS on EC2, you also have: Container Instance Role = ECS Agent permissions on EC2

Execution Role = ECS used. Task Role = application used.

Load Balancer

  • awsvpc: Each task has its own ENI and SG; Available for EC2 tasks and required for Fargate. EC2 bridge/host mode uses the instance's networking.
  • Service registers task to target group. EC2 bridge mode can use dynamic host port mapping; awsvpc uses target type IP. .
  • ECS Service is usually placed as follows: ALB = HTTP / HTTPS = path / host routing
  • NLB = TCP / UDP = high performance
Client
↓
ALB
├── /users  → User Service
└── /orders → Order Service

Auto Scaling

Scaling mechanism on ECSHow it works
Service Auto ScalingApplication Auto Scaling increases/decreases desiredCount by min/max (Target Tracking by CPU, RAM, ALBRequestCountPerTarget; Step Scaling; Scheduled Scaling)
Cluster Auto Scaling (EC2)Capacity Provider connects ECS Cluster with ASG to automatically add EC2 when Pending Tasks lack space (need to attach capacity provider strategy)
targetCapacitySet 100% to take full advantage of EC2 or lower to leave headroom; ASG still needs a valid min/max
ECS on FargateAWS takes care of the entire underlying compute infrastructure; You only configure Service Auto Scaling for the number of Tasks

Storage

If multiple Tasks need to share the same file:

  • S3: = Object Storage = accessible via SDK/API
  • Not a regular shared filesystem.

ECS Compute Modes, Task Placement Strategies & Scheduled Tasks

ECS Compute modeWho manages the Server?Distinguishing point
ECS on FargateAWS completelyServerless per Task (vCPU/RAM), no EC2 management
ECS Managed InstancesAWS operates EC2AWS takes care of provisioning/patching/scaling EC2 but supports choosing specialized machine models & GPUs

Task Placement Strategy (ECS on EC2)How to arrange TasksTarget
spreadSpread Tasks evenly across Availability Zones or EC2 instancesHigh Availability
binpackTightly place Tasks into the fewest EC2 instances according to CPU or MemoryOptimize costs, easily recover idle machines
randomRandomly select EC2 that meets the resource conditionsSimple allocation without priority
  • ECS Scheduled Task: run the container periodically according to cron/rate using Amazon EventBridge Scheduler call RunTask.

4. Practical example

An API runs three ECS tasks behind an ALB; ECS Service replaces a failed task.

↓
ECS
↓
Web API / Microservices
SQS
↓
ECS Worker
↓
Background Job
ECS Tasks
↓
EFS
↓
Shared Files

5. Comparison

  • The task role grants application permissions;
  • the execution role supports task startup tasks such as image pulls and logging.

EC2 vs Fargate

ECS + EC2 = EC2 administrator = instance type + patch + ASG + capacity

ECS + Fargate = AWS manages the server = you only choose CPU/RAM for the Task

Remember:

EC2
→ more controls
→ suitable for stable workload / need to optimize cost

Fargate
→ less infrastructure management
→ suitable for fluctuating workloads
ECS Anywhere and External launch type
  • ECS Anywhere Allows registering servers/VMs on-premises or outside of AWS to the ECS cluster to manage containers using ECS.
  • External launch type (EXTERNAL) used to run ECS tasks/services on these external instances; You still manage the server, OS and capacity.
Server/VM outside of AWS
↓ install ECS Agent + SSM Agent, register cluster
ECS Cluster
↓ launch type: EXTERNAL
Container Tasks on servers outside of AWS

For example, container management processes data at the factory from the ECS while the data remains on-premises.

AWS Fargate

1. Concept

Fargate provides container compute without you creating, patching, or managing the underlying EC2 hosts.

2. Purpose

Reduce provisioning, patching, and host operations for container workloads.

3. How it works

  • AWS Fargate is serverless compute for containers — used with ECS or EKS. You declare the CPU/RAM of task/pod; AWS provision, patch and operate the entire platform server.

Container run flow:

Container image + CPU/RAM
↓ ECS Task / EKS Pod
Fargate
↓
AWS operates compute

What is Fargate not?

  • ECS/EKS: Manage containers: which application to run, how many copies, and when to replace a failed container.
  • Fargate: Provides compute for containers without requiring you to manage EC2 hosts.
  • Together: ECS or EKS manages the work; Fargate runs it. Fargate does not replace ECS/EKS.

What does Fargate still need?

  • VPC + Subnet — each task has its own ENI.
  • Security Group — controls traffic in/out of tasks.
  • IAM role — task execution role + task role.
  • Image — from ECR or other registry.
  • Logs — CloudWatch Logs (via execution role).

Fargate + EFS

  • Fargate task can be mounted Amazon EFS to have persistent shared storage for serverless containers — multiple multi-AZ tasks read/write the same file system.

Limits to remember

  • CPU/RAM must be a supported pair. For example, task 4 vCPU uses 8–30 GB of RAM; This is not Fargate's maximum level. .
  • Cannot SSH into the underlying infrastructure.
  • Some workloads need host-level control (eg custom kernel, GPU) → must use EC2 launch type.

Principles for calculating Compute fees on Fargate

  • Fargate only charges for compute vCPU and Memory allocated during the time the Task/Pod actually runs (don't pay for idle EC2); Expanded storage, data transfer and Load Balancer are charged separately.

4. Practical example

A small operations team runs its API with ECS on Fargate, configuring images, CPU/memory, networking, and scaling.

APIs/microservices run on ECS that the team wants to focus on containers, no Manage EC2 nodes. Job on demand: run tasks when there is work; Use EFS if many tasks need it persistent shared files.

5. Comparison

Fargate provides compute for ECS/EKS; ECS and EKS provide orchestration; ECR stores images.

Fargate vs EC2 launch type

CriteriaFargateEC2
Server managementAWS takes care of everythingYou manage
Charge unitCPU/RAM of taskInstance hours
Infrastructure controlLow (no SSH)High (SSH, custom)
Appropriate workloadSmall, irregular, serverlessLarge, stable, cost-optimized
Amazon ECR

1. Concept

ECR stores container images so applications can pull the intended software package to run.

2. Purpose

Version container images and distribute them to runtime systems with access controls.

3. How it works

  • Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry) is a private Docker/OCI image registry managed by AWS — where container images for ECS, Fargate, or EKS are stored.

Typical usage flow

CI build image → push ECR (tag/digest) → ECS/EKS pull → container runs

Main point

  • Image tag: A readable name such as :latest, which can point to a different image later. Production should use an immutable tag or sha256 digest to pin the intended image.
  • Pull permissions: The task execution role needs the required ECR actions, including ecr:GetAuthorizationToken and ecr:BatchGetImage. IAM and repository policies control push/pull access, especially across accounts.
  • Connectivity: Valid permissions do not fix a missing network path to ECR; check NAT or suitable VPC endpoints.
  • Image scanning: Finds vulnerabilities in images; choose basic scanning or enhanced scanning with Inspector.
  • Lifecycle policy: Automatically removes older images according to rules, such as keeping only the five newest images, to reduce cost.
  • Replication and encryption: Images can be replicated to another Region/account. Stored data uses AWS-managed encryption keys or the selected KMS key.

Security

ClassMechanism
Control planeIAM (create/edit/delete repository)
Data planeRepository policy + IAM (push/pull)
At restSSE with AWS managed key or KMS CMK
In transitTLS (HTTPS)

4. Practical example

CI builds an image, pushes it to ECR, and deploys ECS Service using that image version.

CI build/push image into ECR; ECS/EKS pulls according to digest to deploy the correct version.

Repository cross-account with IAM/repository policy; Scan for vulnerabilities and use lifecycle policy to clean up old images.

5. Comparison

ECR is a registry, not a container runtime; ECS/EKS manage workloads while Fargate or nodes provide compute.

ECR Basic Scanning vs Enhanced Scanning

ECR scan modeScanning Range & Timing
Basic ScanningScan for basic OS packages vulnerabilities when pushing images or upon manual request
Enhanced ScanningIntegration Amazon Inspector to continuously and automatically scan both OS packages and programming language libraries as soon as new CVEs appear
Amazon EKS

1. Concept

EKS runs Kubernetes on AWS; AWS manages the control plane while you deploy applications through Kubernetes APIs.

2. Purpose

Use Kubernetes APIs and its ecosystem without operating the control plane.

3. How it works

  • Amazon EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service) is a Kubernetes control plane managed by AWS — for teams that need Kubernetes APIs, ecosystem (Helm, operators), and portability.

Architecture

AWS-managed control plane (API server · etcd · scheduler)
↓
Workload: EC2 node groups or Fargate pods

Three ways to run workloads on EKS

WayDescriptionWhen to use
Managed node groupsAWS manages your ASG+ node (EC2) for youDefault, simplest
Self-managed nodesYou create/manage EC2 nodes yourselfNeeds maximum control
Fargate profilesPod runs on Fargate (serverless)Don't want to manage the node

IAM and Kubernetes RBAC

🧠 IAM access does not automatically grant Kubernetes permissions. IAM allows you to call the EKS API (create cluster, describe…); but the permissions in the cluster (get pods, deploy...) are due Kubernetes RBAC decide. Need to map IAM principal → Kubernetes role.

Fargate + EKS

  • Fargate can be used with EKS Fargate profile (select namespace/label → pod running on Fargate).
  • EKS is still the control plane layer; Workload must be compatible with Fargate capabilities (eg cannot run daemonset on Fargate).

Node groups, networking & persistent storage

EKS Node Management

  • Managed Node Group: AWS manages the EC2 Nodes and Auto Scaling Group (ASG) lifecycle.

  • Self-Managed Nodes: Self-manage EC2 Nodes, suitable when deep customization is needed.

  • Multi-AZ: Distribute Nodes in Private Subnets in multiple AZs to increase HA.

  • ALB/NLB: Allows application access from outside.

  • NAT Gateway / VPC Endpoints: Let Nodes/Pods access the Internet or AWS Services when needed.

EKS Storage

  • Kubernetes uses PVC (PersistentVolumeClaim) to request storage.
Pod → PVC → StorageClass → CSI Driver
                            |
                            v
                      AWS Storage
                      (EBS / EFS / FSx)

Storage

When to use?

EBS

Block Storage, attached to EC2 Node in the same AZ

EFS

Shared File System multi-AZ, multiple Pods can access at the same time

FSx

HPC, high performance or specialized filesystem

EKS Fargate Limitations

  • Does not support EBS Volume: Use EFS if you need Persistent Shared Storage.

  • Does not support DaemonSet: Does not run workloads that require direct management of the host.

  • No need to manage EC2 Nodes: AWS Fargate provides compute.

Kubernetes resources and permissions for Pods

Organize Kubernetes resourcesNamespaceGroup Pods, Services, Deployments by team/environment

Sensitive application dataKubernetes Secret / AWS Secrets ManagerPasswords, API keys, tokens; Secrets Manager supports rotation when configured

AWS Service needs permission to manage resources for you

Service-Linked Role

EKS Pods need AWS permissions, using OIDC

IRSA

EKS Pods require AWS permissions, simple configuration, do not use OIDC Provider

EKS Pod Identity

EKS runs Pods on Fargate, requiring its own IAM Role

IRSA

ECS Application needs to access S3

ECS Task Role

  • RBAC authorization in Kubernetes; The IAM role grants permissions to AWS. Node roles do not replace individual Pod rights. Restrict Pod access to IMDS to avoid obtaining node credentials.
  • EKS Pod Identity Pods supported on Linux EC2 nodes, Fargate not supported; Fargate Pods can use IRSA. Base64 in a Kubernetes Secret is not encryption; Still need to control access and avoid putting secrets in image/Git. ·

↓ AWS SDK in Pod calls AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity

└── Check IAM Role Trust Policy (issuer, sub, audit)
↓

↓

  • Configuration: IAM OIDC provider for cluster + IAM role with Trust/Permission Policy + Service Account annotation. Trust policy properly limits namespace/Service Account; Use the AWS SDK that supports IRSA.

↓ EKS Auth: AssumeRoleForPodIdentity

  • Configuration: Pod Identity Agent (or integrated EKS Auto Mode) + association between cluster/namespace/Service Account and IAM role. Role trust pods.eks.amazonaws.com with sts:AssumeRole, sts:TagSession; node role needed eks-auth:AssumeRoleForPodIdentity. Use an SDK that supports container credential providers.

Five EKS Compute modes, Autoscaling Streams & Three easily confused Endpoints

EKS Scaling/Compute componentExact role
Kubernetes Metrics ServerCollect actual CPU/RAM of Pods/Nodes assigned to HPA (do not create/delete Pods yourself)
HPA vs VPAHorizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) increase/decrease number of Pods; Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA) Adjust CPU/RAM requests of each Pod
Cluster Autoscaler vs KarpenterCluster Autoscaler increases/decreases nodes via ASG; Karpenter Direct allocation of EC2 optimizes Pending Pods configuration without being tied to a fixed ASG
EKS Auto ModeAWS automatically manages compute, autoscaling, networking, and core add-ons
Load increases → Metrics Server → HPA increases the number of Pods → Lack of space (Pending Pods) → Karpenter / Cluster Autoscaler adds EC2 Nodes
The three Endpoints around EKS are easy to confuseFunction
EKS Interface VPC EndpointCall the AWS EKS Management API (eg DescribeCluster) qua PrivateLink
Kubernetes Private API Endpoint / Kubernetes Public API EndpointWorker Nodes & kubectl communicate with Control Plane (turn on Private Endpoint for pure internal connection; if only enable Kubernetes Public API Endpoint, the private node needs NAT Gateway to join)
ECR Interface Endpoints + S3 Gateway EndpointWorker Nodes in private subnets without the Internet can still pull container images and image layers from S3

4. Practical example

A team using Helm and Kubernetes operators chooses EKS to retain its tools and deployment workflows.

The team already has Kubernetes manifests/Helm charts and needs to keep the Kubernetes API/tooling when moving to AWS.

Stateful pods on EC2 nodes using EBS; multiple pods sharing files or Fargate pods using EFS when appropriate.

5. Comparison

EKS uses Kubernetes; ECS uses AWS orchestration models; choosing orchestration is separate from choosing compute nodes.

EKS vs ECS

CriteriaEKSECS
PlatformKubernetes (open-source, portable)AWS-native, proprietary
ComplexityHigher (learn Kubernetes)Lower, easy to start
EcosystemHelm, operators, tooling K8sLimited to AWS
PortabilityCan run on-prem / other cloudsMainly AWS; ECS Anywhere supports on-premises
When to chooseStandardized K8s, need portabilityWant simple AWS-native

run tasks on external instances; Kubernetes is still the choice when needing the Kubernetes ecosystem.

Summary

  • Amazon ECS: A task definition describes containers; a task is a running copy. ECS Service maintains task count and replaces failures. Task roles grant application permissions.
  • AWS Fargate: Runs containers without managing EC2 hosts. You still choose CPU and memory and configure networking, permissions, and application scaling.
  • Amazon ECR: Stores versioned container images. ECS or EKS pulls them to run applications; ECR itself does not run them.
  • Amazon EKS: Runs Kubernetes with an AWS-managed control plane. Choose suitable pod compute and storage; AWS and Kubernetes permissions are separate layers.

Serverless Overview

AWS Lambda

1. Concept

Lambda runs your code in response to events without you managing the servers that execute it.

2. Purpose

Execute event-driven logic without maintaining an always-on function server.

3. How it works

  • AWS Lambda is used to run code based on events without needing to manage the server.
Event
↓
Lambda
↓
Run Code
↓
Result / Action
  • AWS takes care of itself: Server · Scaling · Runtime infrastructure
  • You mainly worry about: Code · Memory · Timeout · Permissions · Concurrency

Lambda = serverless compute for short-term, event-driven workloads.

Important limits

  • Memory: 128 MB → 10 GB
  • Increasing memory also increases: CPU · Network
  • Timeout: Max execution = 15 minutes
  • Temporary storage: /tmp: 512 MB → 10 GB = temporary / ephemeral

Do not use /tmp to save data long term.

  • Environment variables: Total size · ≈ 4 KB

Lambda Package

  • There are 2 popular ways: ZIP · or · Container Image
1. ZIP
  • Suitable for small application/dependency.
Code + Dependencies
↓
ZIP
↓
Lambda
2. Container Image
Container Image
↓
Lambda Runtime API compatible
↓
Lambda

Lambda containers are not places to run arbitrary Docker. If you need a long-running container or more runtime control → ECS/Fargate is often more suitable.

Event-driven

  • Lambdas are often triggered by: API Gateway · S3 · SQS · EventBridge · DynamoDB Streams · SNS

For example:

S3 Upload
↓
Lambda
↓
Create Thumbnail

Or:

API Gateway
↓
Lambda
↓
Business Logic
↓
Response

Pricing Model

  • Lambda mainly charges according to: Number of Requests + Compute Duration
  • Compute dependencies: Memory · × · Execution Time

Points to remember:

Higher memory
→ More powerful CPU
→ function can run faster
  • Should have the lowest memory not always the cheapest.

Concurrency

  • Concurrency = number of Lambda invocations running at the same time.
50 requests/second

Each request runs 2 seconds
↓
≈ 100 concurrent executions
  • Not: Concurrency ≠ total number of requests

Reserved Concurrency

  • Reserved concurrency: Reserves part of the account's concurrent execution allowance for a function and also caps that function's concurrent executions.
  • Example: A setting of 100 allows up to 100 invocations at once, not 100 requests per second.
  • Purpose: Prevents a function from exhausting account concurrency or overwhelming a downstream database.
  • Comparison: Reserving concurrency does not pre-initialize execution environments; provisioned concurrency does that.
Lambda
↓
RDS
1000 Lambdas
↓
1000 DB Connections
↓
RDS overload

Lambda Layers, SnapStart, Runtime, Memory–CPU Ratio & Hyperplane ENI

Lambda featureThe core point needs to be distinguished
Lambda LayersReuse common libraries/code between multiple functions; each Layer version is immutable — publishing a new version does not automatically change the function pointing to the old version (large data hundreds of MB–GB read/write at runtime, use EFS)
Lambda SnapStartTake a snapshot of the initialized environment to drastically reduce cold starts; only works on published versions (not applicable to $LATEST)
Runtime & Container ImageSupports any language via Custom Runtime and Linux container images up to 10 GB (Windows containers not supported)
Memory ↔ CPU ratioIncreasing RAM will automatically increase CPU scaling and network bandwidth (~1.769 MB equivalent to 1 vCPU; max 10.240 MB ≈ 6 vCPUs; separate from /tmp and 4 KB environment variable limit)
VPC Configuration (Hyperplane ENI)Only turn on when calling private IP (RDS, ElastiCache, Internal EC2, Cross-VPC RDS Proxy); Does not need to be enabled when calling public AWS APIs such as S3/SQS/DynamoDB. Lambda and EC2 and VPC are not required to be in the same subnet

4. Practical example

A new S3 image triggers Lambda to create a thumbnail and save it to a different prefix.

5. Comparison

  • Lambda suits time-bounded event processing;
  • ECS/Fargate suit long-running containers or different runtime requirements.

Lambda vs ECS/Fargate

Lambda = event-driven = short workload = max 15 minutes = self-scaling invocation

ECS / Fargate = container workload = long runs = more runtime control

Resize image
→ Lambda
Backend containers run 24/7
→ ECS / Fargate
Amazon DynamoDB

1. Concept

DynamoDB stores NoSQL key-value or document data on AWS-managed infrastructure, without you operating database servers.

2. Purpose

Serve key-based data access at scale with low infrastructure administration effort.

3. How it works

  • Amazon DynamoDB is a serverless NoSQL database with low latency, self-scaling and no need for server management.
  • DynamoDB = NoSQL = Serverless = Key-value / Document = Millisecond latency
  • Suitable for: User Profile · Shopping Cart · Session · Game Data · IoT Metadata · Serverless API

DynamoDB is strong when it knows access patterns in advance and accesses by key.

Table / Item / Attribute

  • Table = data table
  • Item = a record
  • Attribute = field of record

For example:

Users Table

Item:
{
  UserId: 123,
  Name: "Trong",
  Age: 25
}

Besides the Primary Key, the schema is quite flexible:

Item A
→ Name + Age

Item B
→ Name + Country + Email

Primary Key

  • Primary Key: The key that uniquely identifies an item, chosen when the table is created.
  • Partition Key only: Each key value identifies one item, such as UserId = 123.
  • Partition Key + Sort Key: The pair identifies one item. For example, UserId = 123 and GameId = 1 identifies that user's score in game 1.
  • Memory aid: The partition key groups items; the sort key distinguishes items in that group. One user can have several games with different GameId values.

Item Size

  • Max Item Size = 400 KB
  • Item may contain: String · Number · Boolean · Binary · Null · List · Map · String / Number / Binary Set

Capacity Mode

  • Provisioned: Specify read capacity units (RCUs) and write capacity units (WCUs); suited to predictable demand and can use Auto Scaling.
  • On-Demand: Pay for requests used; suited to unpredictable demand or spikes.
  • Choosing: Use provisioned capacity for direct control over allocated capacity; consider on-demand when reducing advance capacity planning is more important.
Traffic increased
↓
DynamoDB scales itself

Traffic decreased
↓
scale down

  • DAX = in-memory cache specific to DynamoDB.
Application
↓
DAX
↓
DynamoDB

If cache hits:

Application
↓
DAX
↓
Results are very fast

If cache miss:

↓
DynamoDB
↓
get data
↓
cache again
  • DAX can reduce read latency to microseconds.

  • US Region · ↕ · EU Region · ↕ · Asia Region
  • Applications can: Read + Write · in Region near the user
  • Data is replicated between Regions.
  • Global Application · Low Latency · Multi-Region DR

Streams = broadcast change events to consumers. Global Tables = replicate table between Regions.

  • TTL = automatically delete item when expired.
  • Item has attributes: expiresAt = epoch timestamp

  • Session · Token · Temporary Data · Old Events
