5. Comparison

DynamoDB design starts with access patterns and keys; RDS suits relations, joins, and flexible SQL queries.

DAX vs ElastiCache

DAX = DynamoDB cache = DynamoDB API compatible

ElastiCache = general cache = Redis / Valkey / Memcached

DynamoDB reads too much
→ DAX

Cache sessions / aggregates / custom objects
→ ElastiCache

DynamoDB vs RDS

DynamoDB = NoSQL = key-based access = large scale = flexible schema

RDS / Aurora = SQL = relational = joins = complex transactions/business data

Shopping Cart / Session
→ DynamoDB
Order + Customer + Payment
need join/relationship
→ RDS / Aurora

DynamoDB Deletion Protection vs TTL

DynamoDB FeaturesPurpose & Mechanism of action
Deletion ProtectionPrevent accidental deletion of entire DynamoDB tables at the admin level
TTL (Time to Live)Automatically asynchronously purge overdue items based on the epoch timestamp attribute (free WCU at original table, may charge for replication at Global Tables)

AWS Step Functions

1. Concept

Step Functions coordinates services through steps, branches, and error-handling paths.

2. Purpose

Coordinate multiple processing steps with clear status, retry, and error handling.

3. How it works

State machine

  • Workflow includes states such as Task, Choice, Parallel, Map, Wait, Succeed and Fail.
  • Tasks can call Lambda or AWS service integrations, not just connect Lambda to Lambda.
Receive order → validate → inventory/payment → Choice
                                        ├─ success → delivery
                                        └─ error → compensation processing / failure reporting

Retry, Catch and side effects

  • Retry: Automatically tries a step again after a temporary error, such as a busy service.
  • Catch: Routes execution to an error-handling step when work fails; set timeouts to avoid waiting indefinitely.
  • Avoid duplicates: A payment step may run again after a retry. Use a transaction ID to recognize completed requests and avoid charging twice; this is idempotency.
  • Undoing changes: A workflow does not automatically reverse external changes. If an order fails after reserving funds, add a step to refund or release them.

Standard and Express

TypeSuitable
StandardWorkflow is long, needs history/audit; Maximum one year, job/callback support according to integration
ExpressHigh event rate, short tasks; Maximum 5 minutes, mainly request-response
  • Standard uses exactly-once workflow execution according to the service model, but Retry can still call the task again.
  • Asynchronous Express = at-least-once; Synchronous Express is at-most-once.
  • Don't understand execution guarantees as side effect guarantees that never overlap. AWS workflow types.

Human approval

  • Standard workflow can wait for callback/task token for approval or external job.
  • The system sends success/failure results in tokens with appropriate permissions;
  • set timeout and branch when there is no response.
  • The state machine role needs permission to call services; The task's network/permission must still be correct.

4. Practical example

An order passes inventory, payment, and shipping steps; payment failure routes to compensation handling.

Order fulfillment with multiple steps, data processing with parallel/Map, workflow requires human approval. A simple short task that doesn't require additional Step Functions; A multi-state/retry sequence is appropriate.

5. Comparison

Lambda executes code; Step Functions orchestrates state and step order; SQS holds pending work.

  ├─ Low Risk → Allows normal login
  └─ High Risk → Automatically force MFA authentication or block access

CloudFront Functions & Lambda@Edge

1. Concept

CloudFront Functions and Lambda@Edge run code at CloudFront request and response processing stages.

2. Purpose

Perform suitable logic at the edge to reduce unnecessary origin requests.

3. How it works

  • Both are used to run logic right near the user at the CloudFront edge location before/after the request goes to the origin.
  • CloudFront Functions = logic is very light, very fast
  • Lambda@Edge = stronger, more flexible logic

CloudFront Functions = lightweight Lambda@Edge = powerful edge logic

AWS AppSync

1. Concept

AppSync provides GraphQL APIs that connect applications to data sources and real-time updates.

2. Purpose

Build a completely serverless GraphQL API and real-time update channel (Pub/Sub WebSockets), combining multiple data sources in a single query.

3. How it works

Core characteristics

  • AWS AppSync: managed GraphQL & Pub/Sub API services allow clients to retrieve only the necessary data fields from DynamoDB, Aurora/RDS, OpenSearch, Lambda or HTTP APIs through a single endpoint.

4. Practical example

An order screen queries customer and order data through GraphQL and receives status updates through a subscription.

5. Comparison

AppSync focuses on GraphQL and real-time data; API Gateway provides configured REST, HTTP, or WebSocket APIs.

Compare AWS AppSync vs Amazon API Gateway

CriteriaAWS AppSyncAmazon API Gateway
Primary communication modelGraphQL & Real-time Pub/Sub Subscriptions (Pre-managed WebSockets)REST API, HTTP API and the general WebSocket API
Merge multi-source dataResolvers collect data from multiple tables/services in one request, avoiding over-fetchingOften a composite Lambda/backend layer is needed or the client has to call multiple REST endpoints
Authentication supportAPI Key, IAM, Cognito User Pools, OIDC and Lambda AuthorizerIAM, Cognito User Pools, Lambda Authorizer, Mutual TLS

Summary

  • AWS Lambda: Runs code on events without managing servers. Each invocation lasts at most 15 minutes; configure suitable timeouts, resources, and permissions.
  • Amazon DynamoDB: Stores data by key without managing servers. Key design determines how data is retrieved; DAX speeds up suitable read operations.
  • Amazon API Gateway: Receives client API requests and forwards them to backends. It can check authorization and limit traffic; an API key does not replace user authentication.
  • AWS Step Functions: Coordinates steps, retains workflow state, and manages retries. Lambda runs step code; Step Functions orchestrates the overall process.
  • Amazon Cognito: User pools provide registration and sign-in. Identity pools provide temporary AWS credentials with configured permissions.
  • CloudFront Functions & Lambda@Edge: Both run logic at CloudFront's edge. Functions suit very lightweight tasks; Lambda@Edge supports more involved processing with different limits.
  • AWS AppSync: Provides GraphQL APIs for retrieving data from multiple sources and receiving real-time updates such as order status.

Data & Analytics

Amazon Athena

1. Concept

Athena queries data with SQL, commonly reading files directly in S3, without you setting up query servers.

2. Purpose

Analyze data with SQL without loading everything into a database or managing query servers.

3. How it works

  • Amazon Athena is a serverless SQL query service to analyze data primarily located in Amazon S3 without needing to build a database/data warehouse server.
  • Athena = SQL + S3 + Serverless
The file is in S3
↓
Athena
↓
SELECT ... FROM ...
↓
Results

Athena = runs SQL directly on data in S3.

Basic Flow

Producer
↓
S3
↓
Glue Data Catalog
↓
Athena
↓
SQL Query
↓
Query Result
↓
S3
↓
QuickSight / Application
  • In which: S3 = contains real data
  • Glue Data Catalog = table description / schema / partition
  • Athena = run SQL

What can Athena read?

  • Athena can query files such as: CSV · JSON · Parquet · ORC · Avro

For example:

s3://logs/orders/
↓
Athena
↓
SELECT *
FROM orders
WHERE year = 2026;
  • No need to import data into Athena first.

Serverless

  • Athena does not need: Provision server · Choose instance · Manage cluster · Patch server

You just:

There is data in S3
↓
Definition of table/schema
↓
Run SQL

Without a query, there is almost no compute Athena has to run for that query.

Pricing: Data Scanned

  • Athena mainly charges according to: The amount of data scanned by the query
  • According to the slides: ≈ $5 / TB scanned

So:

Scan less
↓
Query is cheaper
+
usually faster

Prices may vary by Region/time.

Data Scanned example

  • Let's say S3 has: 1 TB CSV data
  • Query: SELECT name · FROM users;
  • With CSV, Athena may have to read a lot of file data.

If the same data is saved using Parquet:

  • Athena just needs to read the column "name"
  • → scan less.

Parquet / ORC

  • Parquet and ORC are columnar formats.
  • For example the table has: Name · Age · Country · Salary · Email
  • SELECT Name, Salary · FROM employees;
  • CSV: often have to read the entire row/file
  • Parquet: read only: · Name · Salary
  • → reduce scanned data.
  • Parquet / ORC = less scans = faster = cheaper

Compression

  • Files can be compressed: gzip · Snappy · zstd · ...
Data
↓
Compression
↓
Smaller files
↓
Athena reads fewer bytes
  • → can reduce cost and reading time.

Partition

  • Partition: Groups S3 data by values, such as year=2026/month=09/day=29/.
  • Partition column: A column used to select data groups, such as year, month, or day; Athena has metadata describing those groups.
  • Querying: A suitable partition filter lets Athena read the relevant portion instead of scanning everything.
  • Example: WHERE year = 2026 AND month = 9 reads the September 2026 group when data is partitioned and filtered appropriately.

Partition is Virtual Column

For example, the Parquet file inside does not necessarily contain:

  • But S3 path: /year=2026/month=09/day=29/; Tell Athena: year = 2026 · month = 09 · day = 29

Partition value can come from the S3 path, not necessarily in the file.

If not filter Partition

  • Suppose: 2024/ · 2025/ · 2026/
  • SELECT * · FROM logs;
  • → Athena can scan everything.
  • If: WHERE year = 2026
  • → only scan partition 2026.

Glue + Athena

Raw data:

CSV / JSON
↓
AWS Glue
↓
Convert
↓
Parquet + Partition
↓
S3
↓
Athena
  • This is a very common pattern: Glue = prepare data
  • Athena = query data

Glue Data Catalog

  • Athena needs to know: What is the name of the table? · What columns? · What type of data? · Where is S3 location? · What partition?
  • Glue Data Catalog stores that metadata.

S3 = Data

  • Glue Data Catalog = Metadata
  • Athena = SQL Engine

  • Should avoid: 10 million files is very small; because Athena has to open and process too many objects.
  • Usually better: Fewer files + reasonably large files
  • Suggested slide ranges: > 128 MB / file; But this is a guideline, not a mandatory rule.

5. Comparison

Athena suits ad hoc data-lake queries; Redshift suits warehouses and purpose-tuned BI workloads.

Athena vs Redshift

Athena = direct query S3 = serverless = ad-hoc analytics = pay per data scanned

Redshift = Data Warehouse = data optimized for analytics = query/join/aggregate frequently

Occasionally query logs in S3
→ Athena
BI dashboard runs continuously
+ multiple joins / aggregates
→ Redshift
Amazon Redshift

1. Concept

Redshift is a data warehouse for aggregating and analyzing large datasets with SQL for business reporting.

2. Purpose

Aggregate and analyze large datasets for BI, joins, and OLAP queries.

3. How it works

  • Amazon Redshift is a data warehouse that uses SQL for analytics/BI, suitable for large queries, joins, aggregates and reporting on large amounts of data.
  • Redshift = Data Warehouse = SQL = OLAP
  • Not suitable as the main database for application transactions.
Order / Payment / Account Update
→ RDS / Aurora / DynamoDB

Analytics / Reporting / BI
→ Redshift

Popular flow

RDS / Aurora / DynamoDB / S3
↓
ETL / ELT
↓
Redshift
↓
SQL Analytics
↓
QuickSight / Tableau

For example:

Sales Data
Product Data
Customer Data
↓
Redshift
↓
JOIN + GROUP BY
↓
Sales Dashboard

Why is Redshift fast for Analytics?

  • Redshift is optimized with: Columnar Storage · MPP · Sort Key · Distribution

Columnar Storage

  • Data is stored in columns instead of rows.
  • Table example: Name · Age · Country · Salary
  • Query: SELECT Country, AVG(Salary) · FROM employees · GROUP BY Country;
  • Redshift mainly needs to read: Country · Salary; instead of the entire row.
  • Columnar = scan fewer columns = suitable for analytics

MPP

  • MPP = Massively Parallel Processing
  • Redshift divides the query into multiple compute nodes for parallel processing.
SQL Query
↓
divided into several parts
↓
Compute Node 1
Compute Node 2
Compute Node 3
↓
parallel processing
↓
Result

MPP = multiple machines processing one large query.

Sort Key

  • Sort Key helps data to be sorted by columns commonly used for filtering.
  • Sort Key = OrderDate
  • WHERE OrderDate >= '2026-09-01'
  • → Redshift can skip many unrelated data blocks.
  • Sort Key = reduce the data to be scanned

Distribution

  • Distribution: Determines which Redshift compute nodes store the data.
  • Goal: Spread work evenly and reduce data movement when joining tables.
  • Sort key: Controls data ordering to help find the relevant data faster; it does not assign data to nodes.
Data
↓
Compute Node 1
Compute Node 2
Compute Node 3

Sort Key = how data is sorted. Distribution = which node the data is located at.

Cluster Architecture

In provisioned Redshift:

Client
↓
Leader Node
↓
Compute Nodes
1. Leader Node
  • Leader Node = receive SQL = create query plan = divide work among compute nodes = synthesize results
2. Compute Nodes
  • Compute Nodes = scan data = join = aggregate = parallel processing
QuickSight/Client
↓
Leader Node
↓
split query
↓
Compute Nodes
↓
parallel processing
↓
General leader
↓
Result

Snapshot

  • Snapshot = backup of Redshift cluster at a point in time.
Redshift Cluster
↓
Snapshot
↓
Backup
  • Snapshots are incremental: Headshot = full data
  • Snapshot sau = saves only the changes

Restore Snapshot

  • Restore does not overwrite the old cluster.
Snapshot
↓
Restore
↓
New Redshift Cluster

Restore Snapshot = create new cluster.

Automated Snapshot

  • Redshift automatically creates backups periodically.
Cluster
↓
Automated Snapshot
↓
Retention Window
  • Used for: Regular Recovery · Accidental Change · Operational Backup

Manual Snapshot

You create it yourself:

Redshift
↓
Manual Snapshot
  • Keep it until you delete it.
  • Before migration · Before major change · Long-term backup

Cross-Region Snapshot

You can copy snapshots to another Region:

Region A
Redshift
↓
Snapshot
↓
Copy
↓
Region B
  • Disaster Recovery

If Region A has problems:

Snapshot in Region B
↓
Restore
↓
New Cluster

Multi-AZ

  • Redshift supports Multi-AZ for suitable configurations.
AZ A
+
AZ B
↓
Redshift
  • High Availability
  • Multi-AZ ≠ Cross-Region DR
  • Cross-Region still uses its own snapshot copy/DR strategy.

5. Comparison

Redshift serves OLAP; RDS/Aurora commonly serve OLTP; Spectrum adds S3 data to analytical queries.

OLAP vs OLTP

1. OLTP

Used for application transactions: Create Order · Update User · Payment · Insert / Update small and frequently

Suitable: RDS · Aurora · DynamoDB

2. OLAP

Used to analyze a variety of data: SUM · COUNT · GROUP BY · JOIN · Reporting · Analytics

Redshift

Redshift = OLAP, not OLTP database.

Provisioned vs Serverless

  • Redshift Provisioned: You choose the node type, node count, and processing capacity; suitable for predictable demand and direct capacity control.
  • Your responsibility: Monitor the cluster, plan usage, and resize when needed.
  • Redshift Serverless: AWS provisions and adjusts query processing resources without you managing individual nodes.
  • When to use it: Variable demand, occasional analysis, or teams that do not want to manage a cluster.
Do not manage clusters/nodes directly
↓
AWS provides compute on its own
↓
run query

Redshift vs Athena

Both use SQL for analytics but work differently.

1. Athena

↓

= query S3 directly = serverless = ad-hoc = pay per data scanned

2. Redshift
Data
↓
Load / Model into Warehouse
↓
Redshift
↓
SQL

= data warehouse = optimal for repeated queries = strong with large JOIN / Aggregates

Occasionally query CloudTrail logs
→ Athena
Dashboard sales runs continuously
+ multiple JOIN / GROUP BY
→ Redshift

Redshift Spectrum & Comparison of Provisioned vs Serverless for Data Services

Redshift Spectrum: Use Redshift's computing power to directly query SQL data on S3 (join immediately with Redshift internal tables) without having to first load the entire file into the cluster; If only simple ad-hoc queries on S3 do not have Redshift available, priority is given Athena.

Data servicesProvisioned modeServerless mode
Amazon RedshiftManage the type/number of nodes in the clusterSelf-scaling data warehouse capacity accordingly RPU
Amazon OpenSearchChoose your own domain instances and EBSSelf-scaling search/vector according to OCU
Amazon MSK (Kafka)Choose the number of brokers and disk capacitySelf-scaling throughput streaming, without managing brokers
AWS DMSChoose your own replication instance sizeSelf-scaling between min/max DCU for Full Load & CDC
Amazon OpenSearch Service

1. Concept

OpenSearch builds indexes for fast searches across text and multiple data fields.

2. Purpose

Support flexible multi-field searches and log analysis through search indexes.

3. How it works

  • Amazon OpenSearch Service is a managed search + analytics service, used to create a search index to help search quickly and flexibly across many fields.
  • OpenSearch = Search Engine + Log Analytics + Dashboard

Database keeps original data, OpenSearch keeps index for quick search.

What is OpenSearch used for?

  • Product examples include: Name · Description · Category · Tags · Brand
  • User search: "iphone pro"
  • OpenSearch can: Full-text Search · Partial Match · Filter · Sort · Ranking

For example:

"iph pro"
↓
OpenSearch
↓
iPhone 15 Pro
iPhone 16 Pro

Why not just use DynamoDB?

  • DynamoDB is strong when querying with: Partition Key · Sort Key · Secondary Index
  • But if needed: Search by text · Partial Match · Autocomplete · Search multiple fields
  • then OpenSearch is more suitable.
ProductId = 123
→ DynamoDB
"wireless noise cancelling headphone"
→ OpenSearch

Eventual Consistency

  • Eventual consistency: The search copy may update slightly later than the primary database.
  • Example: A product price changes in DynamoDB while OpenSearch search results briefly show the old price during synchronization.
  • When freshness matters: Read from the appropriate primary source rather than assuming the search copy is immediately current.
DynamoDB Update
↓
Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
OpenSearch Update

  • OpenSearch is also very popular for searching logs.

↓

↓

↓

or:

↓

↓

↓

  • Should: RDS / DynamoDB = save standard data; and: OpenSearch = search / analytics

Lambda vs Firehose Ingest

  • Lambda = flexible transform log = more custom logic
  • Firehose = managed delivery = buffering = less self-management
  • Remember: Lambda path = event-driven / custom transform
  • Firehose path = managed near real-time delivery

Managed vs Serverless

  • There are 2 types of deployment: Provisioned OpenSearch = you choose cluster capacity
  • OpenSearch Serverless = AWS manages scaling/capacity more
  • Provisioned = more control over the cluster
  • Serverless = less infrastructure management
Amazon EMR

1. Concept

EMR runs Spark, Hadoop, and other big-data tools on managed AWS infrastructure.

2. Purpose

Run big-data workloads using supported frameworks and suitable customization.

3. How it works

  • Amazon EMR is a managed service for running big-data processing clusters across multiple EC2 instances.
  • EMR = Spark / Hadoop / HBase / Presto / Flink = big data processing in a distributed fashion
  • AWS takes care of basic provisioning/configuration; You still choose the framework, nodes, and how to optimize your workload.

Node in EMR

  • Primary = cluster coordination = health management / job schedule
  • Core = run task = save data in cluster
  • Task = only runs task = does not keep data
Primary
↓ divide job into multiple tasks
Core + Task Nodes
↓
parallel processing
↓
Output

Primary dispatcher, Core = compute + data, Task = compute only.

Spot / On-Demand

  • On-Demand = stable = not reclaimed like Spot
  • Spot = cheaper = can be terminated
  • Spot is often best suited for: Task Nodes; Because the Task node does not hold data and the task can retry.
  • Primary/Core usually needs more stability.

Auto Scaling

EMR can automatically change the number of nodes according to workload:

Job increases
↓
add node

Job decreased
↓
reduce nodes
  • → reduce costs when workload changes.

Popular flow

S3/HDFS
↓
EMR
↓
Spark/Hadoop Job
↓
Core + Task parallel processing
↓
S3 / Data Store
  • Important data should be saved in sustainable storage such as S3, especially when using Spot or transient clusters.

4. Practical example

A team runs Spark on a large S3 dataset, writes results, and terminates its transient cluster.

Big Data on S3
↓
Spark on EMR
↓
Transform / Feature Engineering
Hadoop / HBase workload
↓
EMR
Large-scale ML preprocessing / Web indexing
↓
EMR Cluster

5. Comparison

EMR suits big-data frameworks and customization; Glue focuses on data integration; Athena focuses on SQL queries.

Long-running vs Transient Cluster

1. Long-running

Cluster is always running

Suitable: Notebook · Interactive workload · HBase · Service needs to operate continuously

2. Transient
Create Cluster
↓
Run Job
↓
Write the results to S3
↓
Terminate Cluster

Suitable for: Batch · Periodic ETL · Spark processing

→ avoid paying when cluster is idle.

EMR vs Athena vs Glue

  • EMR = Spark / Hadoop / Flink = custom big-data code = more cluster control
  • Athena = Ad-hoc SQL directly on S3 = no cluster management
  • Glue = serverless ETL = less infrastructure management

Instance Groups vs Instance Fleets, Managed Scaling & Spark Catalog

Concepts on EMRDistinguishing point
EMR Instance Group vs EMR Instance FleetInstance Group fixes one EC2 type per node group; Instance Fleet Allows mixing many types of EC2 + On-Demand/Spot and manually selecting the available AZ
EMR Managed ScalingAutomatically increase/decrease the number of Core and Task nodes continuously according to the actual load of the job to optimize costs
Apache Spark vs Spark CatalogSpark is a distributed processing engine; spark_catalog (usually integrated Glue Data Catalog) only manages table/schema metadata, not original data
Amazon QuickSight

1. Concept

QuickSight creates charts and interactive dashboards from business data.

2. Purpose

Turn data into dashboards and reports that business users can consume.

3. How it works

Amazon QuickSight is a serverless Business Intelligence (BI) service for creating:

  • Charts · Reports · Interactive Analysis · Dashboards
  • QuickSight is used to visualize data and create insights, not a database, data warehouse or ETL engine.
  • QuickSight = BI = Visualization = Dashboard

Where does QuickSight get its data?

  • Can connect to: RDS · Aurora · Athena · Redshift · S3 · OpenSearch · Timestream
  • There are also: CSV / XLSX / JSON / TSV · Salesforce / Jira · On-prem database qua JDBC
Data Source
↓
QuickSight
↓
Charts / Dashboard

Direct Query

  • QuickSight can directly query the data source.
QuickSight
↓
Redshift / Athena / RDS

Each time the dashboard needs data:

QuickSight
↓
query source
↓
get results
  • Advantages: Newer data
  • Disadvantages: Depends on source speed · Increase source load

SPICE

  • SPICE = QuickSight's in-memory engine.
Data Source
↓
Import
↓
SPICE
↓
Dashboard
  • QuickSight queries the imported data in memory instead of continuously querying the source.
  • Benefits: Faster dashboard · Reduced database load

In return:

Data is not automatically always fresh
↓
need refresh

Sharing

  • Dashboards can: Share with QuickSight User · Share with QuickSight Group · Embed into application
Redshift
↓
QuickSight
↓
Sales Dashboard
↓
Embed into the company portal
  • QuickSight users/groups is the identity within QuickSight.
  • QuickSight Group ≠ IAM Group

Column-Level Security

  • Enterprise edition supported Column-Level Security (CLS).
  • Table example: Name · Department · Salary · Email
  • HR: Sales user: Name · Department · Email
  • don't see: Salary

CLS = controls which columns the user can view.

Row-Level Security

  • In addition to columns, you can limit by row.
User Singapore
→ only see Region = Singapore

User Vietnam
→ only see Region = Vietnam
  • RLS = controls which rows the user can see
  • Remember: CLS = Column

RLS = Row

Security Layers

There are two different types of permissions:

QuickSight → Data Source
  • QuickSight needs permission/credential to read the database.

And:

User → QuickSight Dashboard
  • QuickSight user/group decides who gets to see the dashboard.

Having permission to view the dashboard does not mean that the user directly has access to the source database.

What doesn't QuickSight do?

  • QuickSight is not: Data Warehouse · ETL Engine · Transactional Database
  • Glue = transform data
  • Redshift = warehouse
  • Athena = query data
  • QuickSight = visualize results

4. Practical example

Sales teams view revenue dashboards; row-level security limits each region to its own data.

5. Comparison

QuickSight visualizes data; Athena/Redshift query and analyze it; SPICE and direct queries differ in caching and freshness.

Analysis vs Dashboard

  • Analysis: The workspace where an author builds and edits charts, filters, parameters, controls, and sorting.
  • Dashboard: A published version of an analysis shared with viewers.
  • Viewers: Can filter and drill into data within the published dashboard's scope without editing the source analysis.
  • Example: An analyst builds revenue charts in an analysis, then publishes a dashboard for managers to view by month or region.
Sales Analysis
├── Revenue chart
├── Region filter
├── Date filter
└── Product chart
Analysis
↓
Publish
↓
Dashboard

Direct Query vs SPICE

Direct Query = query source directly = fresher data = depends on source

SPICE = import copy into QuickSight = faster = need to refresh

Direct Query = prioritize freshness. SPICE = performance priority.

AWS Glue & Lake Formation

1. Concept

Glue discovers and prepares data; Lake Formation centrally manages access to a data lake.

2. Purpose

Prepare data, maintain metadata, and centrally govern data-lake access.

3. How it works

  • AWS Glue provides serverless ETL and metadata catalog to prepare data for analytics;
  • AWS Lake Formation builds a data lake management layer on Glue to ingest, catalog, and centrally control access to data typically stored in S3.
  • AWS Glue = ETL + Data Catalog
  • Lake Formation = Governance + Permission for Data Lake
  • S3 = contains data
  • Glue = learn data + transform data
  • Lake Formation = control who can see the data

AWS Glue

  • Glue is the service serverless ETL.
Extract
↓
Transform
↓
Load

For example:

Raw CSV/JSON on S3
↓
Glue
↓
Clean + Convert Parquet + Partition
↓
Curated Data on S3
↓
Athena / Redshift / QuickSight

Glue Crawler

  • Glue Crawler: Inspects data to discover columns, data types, partitions, and storage locations.
  • Sources: Reads supported sources such as S3, RDS, DynamoDB, or JDBC connections.
  • Output: Stores metadata in the Glue Data Catalog so other tools know how to read the data.
  • Example: A crawler finds id, price, and date columns in order files and creates a table description. It does not transform the order files.

Glue Data Catalog

  • Glue Data Catalog: A directory describing data: table names, columns, data types, partitions, and storage locations.
  • Actual data: Remains at the source, such as S3; the catalog stores descriptions, called metadata.
  • Example: An orders table has id, price, and date columns and points to s3://data/orders/.
  • Consumers: Athena, Redshift Spectrum, EMR, and Glue use the catalog to locate and read data.

Job Bookmark

  • Job Bookmark remembers which data has been processed.
Run 1
→ process files A and B

Run 2
→ only processes C files

Instead:

A + B + C
→ reprocess everything

Bookmark = avoid reprocessing old data.

  • It should not be understood as an exactly-once guarantee for every pipeline.

Glue Studio & DataBrew

  • Glue Studio = GUI create/run/monitor Glue Jobs
  • DataBrew = low-code/no-code data cleaning
  • DataBrew is suitable for: Remove duplicate · Normalize · Clean values · Standardize data

Glue Streaming ETL

  • In addition to batch ETL, Glue can handle streams.
Kinesis / Kafka / MSK
↓
Glue Streaming ETL
↓
Transform
↓
S3 / Analytics Target
  • It uses: Apache Spark Structured Streaming

AWS Lake Formation

  • Lake Formation is used to manage and protect Data Lake.
  • Lake Formation = Data Lake Governance
  • Usually data is still located in: Amazon S3
  • Lake Formation is not new storage.

Fine-Grained Access

Lake Formation can limit permissions to:

  • Database · Table · Column · Row

Table example:

Employee
├── Name
├── Department
└── Salary
  • HR: Name ✅ · Department ✅ · Salary ✅
  • Analyst: Name ✅ · Department ✅ · Salary ❌
  • Column-Level Access

Rows can be limited:

Vietnam Team
→ only see Region = Vietnam

Lake Formation does not replace IAM/S3/KMS

  • Lake Formation is an additional layer of governance.
  • You may still need: IAM · S3 permissions · KMS permissions · Network access

Example file using SSE-KMS:

User
↓
Lake Formation allows tables
↓
but no KMS permission
↓
Still can't read data

Full Flow Data Lake

S3 / RDS / DynamoDB

Glue Crawler
↓
Glue Data Catalog
↓
Glue ETL
↓
Curated S3 Data Lake
↓
Lake Formation
↓
Fine-Grained Permission
↓
Athena / Redshift / EMR
↓
QuickSight

Streaming platforms: Amazon MSK & Managed Service for Apache Flink

1. Concept

MSK runs Kafka to store and transport streams; Flink processes streaming data while maintaining processing state.

2. Purpose

Operate Kafka streams and stateful processing without building the entire platform.

3. How it works

  • Amazon MSK is managed Apache Kafka to stream event streams by topic/partition;
  • Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink (formerly Kinesis Data Analytics for Apache Flink) runs Flink managed applications to process real-time streams from Kinesis Data Streams or MSK.
  • Amazon MSK = Managed Apache Kafka = receive and transmit event stream
  • Managed Service for Apache Flink = real-time stream processing
Producer
↓
MSK / Kafka
↓
Flink
↓
Transform / Aggregate / Detect
↓
Sink

Amazon MSK

  • Amazon MSK is Apache Kafka is managed by AWS.
  • Instead of self: Install Kafka · Manage broker · Patch · Recovery · Scaling
  • AWS manages the infrastructure for you.
  • MSK = Kafka API + Kafka ecosystem + Managed infrastructure
  • Clusters usually run in VPC and can span multiple AZs to increase HA.

Kafka Model

  • Kafka has 3 important components: Producer = send event
  • Topic = stream / event type
  • Consumer = read event

For example:

Order Service
↓ Producer
Topic: orders
↓
Fraud Consumer
Analytics Consumer
Notification Consumer
  • An event stream can be processed independently by multiple consumers.

Topic & Partition

  • Topics are divided into: Partitions
orders topic
├── Partition 0
├── Partition 1
└── Partition 2
  • Partition helps: Parallel Processing · Scale throughput

Consumer Group can divide partitions among multiple consumers:

Partition 0 → Consumer A
Partition 1 → Consumer B
Partition 2 → Consumer C

Topic = logical stream, Partition = parallelism unit.

  • Kafka can add partition, but does not reduce partition count directly.

Producer & Consumer is still your code

  • MSK manages the Kafka infrastructure, but the business logic is still handled by the application.
Producer Code
↓
MSK
↓
Consumer Code
↓
Business Logic
  • MSK does not understand: Order · Payment · Fraud · Customer; it just transports/saves event.

Storage & Replication

Kafka records are saved according to retention:

Producer
↓
Broker
↓
EBS Storage

Broker can replicate data:

Broker A
↓ replicate
Broker B
↓
Broker C
  • → increase durability and availability.

MSK Serverless

  • Two main ways: Provisioned MSK = select/manage broker capacity
  • MSK Serverless = AWS self-provisioning + scale capacity
  • Serverless is suitable when: Traffic changes a lot · Don't want capacity planning
  • Provisioned is suitable when more cluster/capacity control is needed.

Managed Service for Apache Flink

  • Flink is used for Process data while the stream is running.
Kinesis / MSK
↓
Flink
↓
Transform
Filter
Aggregate
Join
Window
↓
Output
  • Can be written with: Java · Scala · SQL

Flink example

Event:

User A → $100
User A → $500
User A → $3000

Flink can:

Group by User
↓
total in 5 minutes
↓
Abnormal detection
↓
Fraud Alert
  • Here it is stateful stream processing.

Flink State & Checkpoint

  • Flink can remember state between events.
  • User A total = $600
  • New event: +$3000
  • Flink continues from the old state: Total = $3600

To recover:

State
↓
Checkpoint / Snapshot
↓
Failure
↓
Restore State

Checkpoint = saves state so Flink can continue after failure.

Auto Scaling & Parallelism

Flink can parallelize:

Stream
↓
Worker 1
Worker 2
Worker 3

When throughput increases:

Auto Scaling
↓
More Compute
  • But correctness still depends: Checkpoint · Offsets · Duplicate handling · Late events · Sink idempotency

Where does Flink read from?

  • According to slide content: Kinesis Data Streams ✅ · Amazon MSK ✅ · Data Firehose ❌
  • Remember: KDS / MSK = stream source
  • Flink = stream processor

MSK Consumer

  • MSK can be consumed by: Managed Flink · Glue Streaming ETL · Lambda · EC2 / ECS / EKS Application
Application
↓
MSK
├── Flink → Real-time Analytics
├── Lambda → Business Logic
└── Glue → S3 Data Lake

4. Practical example

Transaction events enter Kafka on MSK; a Flink application calculates spending totals within time windows.

many independent consumers

5. Comparison

MSK is a Kafka streaming platform; Flink is a processing engine; Kinesis Data Streams is an AWS-native streaming option.

Kinesis Data Streams vs MSK

Kinesis Data Streams = AWS-native streaming = uses Shards

MSK = Apache Kafka = use Topics + Partitions

Kinesis
→ want AWS-native, less interested in Kafka ecosystem
MSK
→ app used Kafka
→ needs Kafka APIs / ecosystem

Shard vs Partition

Kinesis = Shard

Kafka / MSK = Partition

Both are used to: chia workload + parallel processing

And we all need to choose a reasonable partition key to avoid:

Hot Partition / Hot Shard

Serverless big-data ingestion pipeline

1. Concept

An ingestion pipeline separates event intake, storage, preparation, and analysis into stages.

2. Purpose

Separate collection, storage, and analysis so a pipeline can scale and handle failures clearly.

3. How it works

  • This is a pattern to receive real-time events → save to S3 → query with Athena → create dashboard/report without having to manage the ingestion cluster yourself.
IoT Devices
↓
IoT Core
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Data Firehose
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
QuickSight / Redshift

Role of each service

  • IoT Core = receive events from IoT devices
  • Kinesis Data Streams = streaming ingestion real-time
  • Data Firehose: buffer + delivery managed → usually writes to S3
  • S3 = save raw / processed data
  • Athena = SQL query on S3
  • QuickSight = dashboard / visualization
  • Redshift = warehouse analytics if large queries/joins are needed frequently

Full flow

IoT Devices
↓
IoT Core
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Data Firehose
↓
Lambda Transform (optional)
↓
S3 Ingestion Bucket
↓
SQS (optional)
↓
Lambda
↓
Athena Query
↓
S3 Reporting Bucket
↓
QuickSight / Redshift

Kinesis Data Streams

  • KDS receives data real-time.
Devices / Applications
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
continuous records

For example:

  • Temperature event · Click event · Application log · Telemetry
  • Need to choose: Partition Key; reasonable to avoid hot shards.

Data Firehose

  • Firehose took the stream buffer and delivery to destination.
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Firehose
↓
S3
  • Firehose can: Buffer records · Batch records · Retry delivery · Transform using Lambda
  • Because there is buffering: Firehose = Near Real-Time; Not every event that arrives immediately appears in S3.

Can add:

↓

↓
S3
Raw JSON
↓

  • Lambda here is optional.

S3 + SQS + Lambda

When Firehose writes the file to S3:

S3 Event
↓
SQS
↓
Lambda
  • SQS is used to: Buffer · Retry · Decouple · Handle traffic spike

If the pipeline is simple:

S3
↓
Lambda

also.

Why add SQS?

Without SQS:

S3 Event
↓
Lambda

Yes SQS:

S3 Event
↓
SQS Queue
↓
Lambda

If Lambda is slow:

Message
→ wait in SQS
  • instead of applying pressure directly to Lambda.

SQS = buffer + decouple + retry.

SQS does not guarantee exactly-once

  • Reprocessing: A message or event may be received and processed more than once.
  • Idempotent: Repeating the same request produces only the intended single result.
  • Example: Use a file ID or job ID to recognize completed work and avoid duplicate rows or two reports for one file.
Same Event
↓
Lambda
↓
can run multiple times

Athena Query

Lambda can trigger Athena:

Lambda
↓
Start Athena Query
↓
Athena reads data from S3
↓
SQL
  • SELECT device_id, AVG(temperature) · FROM telemetry · GROUP BY device_id;
  • Athena saves the result to: S3 Reporting Bucket

Query Result is in S3

Athena
↓
Query Result
↓
S3

Then:

QuickSight
↓
Dashboard

or:

Reporting Data
↓
Redshift
↓
Warehouse Analytics

Clickstream flows through Firehose into S3, Glue catalogs it, and Athena supports dashboard queries.

5. Comparison

  • Real-time stream processing and reports over buffered data have different latency; choose according to freshness requirements.

Real-Time vs Near Real-Time

Kinesis Data Streams = Real-Time Streaming

Firehose = Buffer + Delivery = Near Real-Time

Event occurs
↓
KDS received it immediately
↓
Firehose buffer
↓
Then write the batch to S3

Therefore, the entire pipeline to the dashboard is usually not strict real-time.

QuickSight vs Redshift at the end of the pipeline

Athena / S3
↓
QuickSight

Use when: Dashboard · Reporting · Visualization

If needed: many JOINs · large aggregates · continuously repeated queries

then:

S3
↓
Redshift
↓
QuickSight

may be more suitable.

AWS Data Pipeline, Amazon MWAA & AWS Data Exchange

1. Concept

Data Pipeline and MWAA orchestrate data tasks; Data Exchange provides datasets from external providers.

2. Purpose

Orchestrate complex scheduled/dependent data processing workflows and integrate third-party data sets to AWS.

3. How it works

AWS Data Exchange

  • AWS Data Exchange: data marketplace helps subscribe to commercial/public datasets from third-party providers and receive them directly into S3, Redshift, Lake Formation or via API without having to write your own collection pipeline.

4. Practical example

A team uses MWAA for a daily DAG and includes subscribed Data Exchange datasets in reports.

5. Comparison

  • MWAA orchestrates Airflow DAGs;
  • Data Exchange distributes datasets;
  • Data Pipeline is a legacy service with availability considerations.

Amazon MWAA vs AWS Data Pipeline

ServiceRoles & Distinguishing Points
Amazon MWAA (Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow)Run environment Apache Airflow managed to orchestrate multi-step data pipelines using Python code (DAGs), handling dependencies, retries, and warnings; MWAA itself coordination only, while computation/storage is handled by S3, Glue, EMR, Redshift
AWS Data PipelineService that coordinates periodic data migration and transformation between AWS/on-premises data warehouses according to dependencies
Amazon MWAA (scheduled DAG) → enable AWS Glue ETL read S3 → load Amazon Redshift table → send SNS notification

Summary

  • Amazon Athena: Queries S3 data with SQL without provisioning query servers. Parquet files and partitions help reduce scanned data.
  • Amazon Redshift: A warehouse for large-scale SQL analysis. Suits revenue reporting rather than being the default database for individual order transactions.
  • Amazon OpenSearch: Indexes data for search and log analysis. It commonly receives copies from a primary source, so results can lag behind that source.
  • Amazon EMR: Runs big-data tools such as Spark and Hadoop. Useful when their frameworks and customization are required.
  • Amazon QuickSight: Turns data into charts and dashboards using sources such as Athena or Redshift. It is not the primary data store.
  • AWS Glue & Lake Formation: Glue discovers schemas and transforms data. The Data Catalog stores metadata; Lake Formation governs who can access which lake data.
  • Amazon MSK & Apache Flink: MSK runs Kafka to retain and transport events. Flink processes incoming events, such as calculating totals over time windows.
  • Data Ingestion Pipeline: Separates collection, storage, preparation, and analysis. Each service has a role; the design determines freshness and failure handling.
  • MWAA, Data Pipeline & Data Exchange: MWAA schedules Airflow workflows. Data Exchange provides third-party datasets. Data Pipeline is a legacy service; check availability before using it.

Amazon Lex & Amazon Connect

1. Concept

Lex builds chat or voice bots; Connect provides contact-center infrastructure and customer-service flows.

2. Purpose

Automate customer intake and route requests to appropriate bots or agents.

3. How it works

  • Amazon Lex is used to create chatbots/voice bots that understand user intentions, while Amazon Connect is a cloud contact center to receive calls and coordinate contact flow.
  • Lex = understand speech/text = recognize intent
  • Connect = receive calls = coordinate contact flow

Amazon Lex

Lex uses:

= Speech → Text

= understand what the user wants to do

Lex confidence is low

  • Lex understands the request: The bot identifies what the user wants, such as changing a delivery address; it does not decide whether the user is authorized.
  • Backend checks: Validate input, user permissions, and business rules before changing data.

Different from available AI services such as Translate or Textract:

SageMaker Batch Transform vs Real-Time Endpoint

Inference modeMechanisms & Use Cases
SageMaker Batch TransformRun offline batch inference on large data sets stored in S3 and then write the results to S3, automatically turn off compute when the job is finished — used when immediate results are not needed for each request (not used for ETL data cleaning)
SageMaker Real-Time EndpointMaintain a continuously running HTTPS endpoint to deliver predictable low latency (milliseconds) for each online request
Amazon Kendra & Amazon Personalize

1. Concept

Kendra searches internal documents for answers; Personalize recommends content or products from user behavior.

Summary

  • Rekognition & Textract: Rekognition identifies image and video content. Textract extracts text, form fields, and tables from documents.
  • Transcribe, Polly & Translate: Transcribe converts speech to text. Polly speaks text aloud. Translate converts text between languages.
  • Lex & Connect: Lex builds bots that understand requests and collect information. Connect runs contact centers and can use Lex before routing callers to agents.
  • Comprehend & Comprehend Medical: Comprehend extracts information and sentiment from text. Comprehend Medical focuses on supported medical information such as conditions, medications, and treatments.
  • Amazon SageMaker AI: Helps build models from your own data, train them, and use them in applications. Suits custom models rather than a prebuilt AI API.
  • Kendra & Personalize: Kendra finds information in enterprise documents. Personalize recommends products or content using individual user behavior.

Monitoring, Audit & Observability

Amazon CloudWatch: metrics, logs, alarms & insights

1. Concept

CloudWatch collects metrics and logs to show how systems are running and alert on configured conditions.

2. Purpose

Amazon CloudWatch is used to monitor the AWS system with metrics, logs, dashboards and alarms.

3. How it works

  • CloudWatch = Metrics + Logs + Dashboard + Alarms
  • It replied: "How is the system running?"
  • Other than: CloudTrail = who called which AWS API?

Functional groups and EventBridge

Functional groupWhat to use?
MetricsCollect measurements over time: CPU, latency, request count
LogsCentralize application/service logs
DashboardsDisplays graphs and system status
AlarmsMonitor conditions/status for alerts or action calls
InsightsAnalyze logs and observe specialized workloads such as containers/Lambda
CloudWatch Events → Amazon EventBridgeReceive events, use filtering rules and pass to targets like Lambda/SQS
  • These are functional groups, not mutually exclusive types of monitoring. CloudWatch Events is the former name of Amazon EventBridge; Alarm tracks metrics, EventBridge routes events such as EC2 state changes or state change alarms.

CloudWatch Metrics

  • Metric = a value measured over time.

For example:

  • CPUUtilization · NetworkIn · RequestCount · ErrorCount
EC2 / Lambda / RDS...
↓
Metrics
↓
CloudWatch
↓
Dashboard / Alarm

Namespace & Dimension

  • Namespace: A group for a service or application's metrics, such as AWS/EC2, AWS/Lambda, or AWS/RDS.
  • Dimension: Information distinguishing a particular resource's metrics, such as InstanceId = i-123456.
  • Example: Two EC2 instances both report CPUUtilization, but InstanceId lets you view each instance's CPU separately.

EC2 granularity and RDS metrics

Resources/modeGranularityData
EC2 Basic Monitoring5 minutes for most metrics; status checks 1 minuteEC2 metrics default
EC2 Detailed Monitoring1 minuteEC2 metrics; Need to turn on, there is a fee
Default RDS CloudWatch1 minuteDatabase metrics; No need for EC2 Detailed Monitoring
RDS Enhanced MonitoringCan be down to 1 secondOS/process metrics → CloudWatch Logs; Need to enable and monitor role
  • EC2 RAM needs Agent/custom metric. Enhanced Monitoring collection 1 second does not mean the RDS console refreshes every second. ·

Custom Metrics

  • You can manually send metrics to CloudWatch.

Important examples:

Default EC2
→ has CPU / Network...

RAM Usage
→ no default
→ need Agent / Custom Metric

CloudWatch Dashboard

Metrics / Logs
↓
CloudWatch Dashboard
↓
Graph / Chart / Status
  • Dashboard example: CPU · Memory · Request Count · Errors · Latency

CloudWatch Logs

  • CloudWatch Logs is used to centralize logs from application/service.
  • Popular sources: Lambda · ECS · API Gateway · VPC Flow Logs · CloudTrail · EC2 · On-premises
1. EC2 / On-premises
  • Logs are not automatically sent to CloudWatch.
EC2
↓
CloudWatch Agent
↓
CloudWatch Logs
  • Agents need: Config + IAM Permission

CloudWatch Logs Insights

  • Logs Insights = query/search log saved in CloudWatch Logs.
CloudWatch Logs
↓

Purpose:

  • Reduce alarm noise

  • System Status = underlying problem AWS host
  • EBS Status = attached storage problem

  • → AWS moves the instance to more powerful hardware.

CloudWatch Insights

  • Some specialized features: Container Insights = monitor ECS / EKS / Containers
  • Lambda Insights = detailed Lambda monitor
  • Contributor Insights = find top contributors like the IP sending the most traffic/errors
  • Application Insights = monitor application and dependency
  • No need to remember too much, just remember that these are specialized monitoring tools of CloudWatch.

Network Monitor

  • Used to monitor the network between AWS and on-premises.
  • AWS · ↕ · Direct Connect / VPN · ↕ · On-premises
  • Follow: Latency · Packet Loss · Jitter

Amazon EventBridge

1. Concept

EventBridge receives events, filters them with rules, and routes them to services that should react.

2. Purpose

Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus used to receive events, filter events, and automatically send events to other services.

3. How it works

  • EventBridge = receive event + filter event + route event to target
"Something happened"
↓
EventBridge detected
↓
"Then do something"

Basic Flow

Event Source
↓
EventBridge Bus
↓
Rule
↓
Target

For example:

EC2 is stopped
↓
EventBridge
↓
Rule match
↓
Lambda / SNS / SQS

Rule decides when to run, Target is the service that actually works.

Event Source

  • EventBridge can receive events from: AWS Services · SaaS Applications · Custom Applications · CloudTrail
  • EC2 state change · S3 event · CodeBuild failed · CloudTrail API call · Custom application event

Rule

Rules have 2 main types:

1. Event Pattern
  • Runs when an event meets the conditions.
Event occurs
↓
Rule checks JSON
↓
Match?
↓
Target
EC2 State = stopped
↓
EventBridge Rule
↓
SNS Alert
2. Schedule / Cron
  • Run over time.
Every 1 hour
↓
EventBridge
↓
Lambda
00:00 every day
↓
EventBridge
↓
Lambda cleanup temporary files

Target

  • Target is the service that handles events after the rule match.
  • Could be: Lambda · SQS · SNS · Step Functions · ECS · AWS Batch · Kinesis · CodePipeline · CodeBuild · SSM
Order Created
↓
EventBridge
↓
SQS
↓
Order Worker

Event Bus

  • EventBridge has the following bus types: Default Event Bus = AWS service events
  • Partner Event Bus = SaaS like Datadog/Zendesk
  • Custom Event Bus = event from your application
My Application
↓
Custom Event Bus
↓
Rule
↓
Lambda

Producer & Consumer separate

EventBridge key points:

Producer
↓
EventBridge
↓
Consumer
  • The producer does not need to know who handles the event.
Order Service
↓
"OrderCreated"
↓
EventBridge
├── Inventory Lambda
├── Email SQS
└── Analytics Kinesis

Order Service only broadcasts events, no need to call each service directly.

CloudTrail + EventBridge

  • CloudTrail records AWS API activity.
  • EventBridge can react to those events.
User calls DeleteTable
↓
CloudTrail records
↓
EventBridge Rule match
↓
SNS
↓
Security Team

Or:

Root Login
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS Alert

EventBridge react after the event occurs, do not block previous API calls.

Archive & Replay

  • Archive: Stores EventBridge events for later use.
  • Replay: Sends stored events again, such as testing a new consumer or reprocessing after a fix.
  • Consumer: Still needs retry and duplicate-effect handling because an old event may be processed again.
Event Bus
↓
Archive
Archive
↓
Replay
↓
Rule / Target

Replay does not replace consumer idempotency/retry logic.

Schema Registry

  • Schema Registry = saves/understands the event JSON structure

Event example:

{
  "orderId": "123",
  "status": "PAID"
}
  • Schema helps the application know what fields the event has.

Cross-account

  • EventBridge can send events between AWS accounts.
Account A
↓
EventBridge
↓
Account B Central Event Bus
  • Suitable for: Multi-account AWS Organization · Central Monitoring · Central Security Automation
  • Need: IAM Permission + Event Bus Resource Policy

4. Practical example

An order-cancellation event triggers a refund workflow without the order service directly calling every system.

Every 1 hour
↓
EventBridge
↓
Lambda
EC2 State Change
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS Alert
CloudTrail DeleteTable
↓
EventBridge
↓
Security Notification
OrderCreated
↓
EventBridge
↓
SQS / Lambda / Step Functions

5. Comparison

An event bus routes events; Scheduler triggers time-based work; SQS holds messages for consumers.

AWS CloudTrail

1. Concept

CloudTrail records API activity and selected events to show who did what in AWS.

2. Purpose

AWS CloudTrail is used to audit activity in an AWS account.

3. How it works

  • It replied: Who? · Doing what? · When? · From where?

For example:

EC2 is terminated
↓
CloudTrail
↓
Which User/Role calls the API?
Which IP?
What time?
Which API?

CloudTrail = audit AWS API activity.

What does CloudTrail record?

  • CloudTrail records activity from: AWS Console · CLI · SDK · AWS Services · IAM User / Role
  • CreateSubnet · DeleteBucket · TerminateInstances · AttachRolePolicy

Event History

  • CloudTrail does Event History default for management events.
  • Event History = see recent activity = keep for about 90 days

If you need to save longer:

CloudTrail Trail
↓
S3
↓
Athena query

Trail

  • Trail = configure CloudTrail to send long-term logs to storage.
CloudTrail
↓
Trail
↓
S3
  • You can send more to: CloudWatch Logs
  • Trails can: Single Region · or · All Regions

Types of Events

1. Management Events
  • Is an AWS resource management operation.
  • CreateSubnet · AttachRolePolicy · CreateTrail · TerminateInstances
  • Can be divided: Read = read only
  • Write = change resources

Management events are the most common type when investigating who edited/deleted a resource.

Data Events

  • Is an activity directly on data/object/function.
  • S3 GetObject · S3 PutObject · S3 DeleteObject · Lambda Invoke
  • Data events: High volume · Higher cost · No default logging
  • So it's usually only enabled for important resources.

CloudTrail Insights

  • CloudTrail Insights is used for discovery unusual activity.
Normal:
10 API calls/minute

Suddenly:
1000 API calls/minute
↓
CloudTrail Insights
↓
Anomaly
  • Can detect: Abnormal IAM activity · Abnormal Provisioning · API burst · Behavior different from baseline
Management Write Events
↓
CloudTrail Insights
↓
Detect anomaly
↓
EventBridge / Alert

Investigation with S3 + Athena

If saving CloudTrail to S3:

CloudTrail
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
SQL Query
  • Example you want to find: Who deletes bucket? · Who terminates EC2? · Who edits Security Group?

CloudTrail + EventBridge

  • CloudTrail logs events.
  • EventBridge can react to that event.
User calls DeleteTable
↓
CloudTrail records
↓
EventBridge matches
↓
SNS/Lambda/SQS

CloudTrail = record

EventBridge = response

Overall flow

User / Role / AWS Service
↓
API Call
↓
CloudTrail
↓
Management / Data / Insights Event
↓
Event History / S3 / CloudWatch Logs
↓
Athena / EventBridge
↓
Investigation / Alert / Automation

4. Practical example

After a security group is opened, security investigates the API call, identity, and time in CloudTrail.

EC2 is terminated
↓
CloudTrail
↓
Find user + API + IP + time
Sensitive S3 Bucket
↓
Enable Data Events
↓
Log GetObject / PutObject / DeleteObject
CloudTrail
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
Long-term audit
Sensitive API Call
↓
CloudTrail
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS Alert

5. Comparison

  • CloudTrail shows who did what;
  • CloudWatch shows how systems operate;
  • Config shows how configurations changed.

CloudTrail vs CloudWatch

CloudTrail = who did what on AWS?

CloudWatch = How is the system working?

EC2 is deleted
→ CloudTrail

EC2 CPU = 95%
→ CloudWatch
AWS Config & compliance automation

1. Concept

AWS Config records resource configuration over time and checks it against configured rules.

2. Purpose

AWS Config is used to monitor the configuration of AWS resources over time and check whether the resource complies with the rules or not.

3. How it works

  • AWS Config = Configuration History + Compliance Check
  • It replied: How are resources configured? · What was the previous configuration like? · Is it compliant with the rule?

Easy to understand example

  • You have rule: Security Group · do not open SSH 22 for 0.0.0.0/0
  • If there is SG: Port 22 · Source = 0.0.0.0/0
  • Config rating: NON_COMPLIANT ❌
  • If only for corporate IP: Port 22 · Source = Company IP

→

COMPLIANT ✅

Config = check if the configuration has the correct policy.

What does AWS Config track?

For example:

  • Security Group · S3 Bucket · EC2 · EBS · ALB · IAM · ...
  • May know: Current configuration · Previous configuration · When the configuration changed · Compliance status

Resource Timeline

  • Config saves the resource's change history.
  • 10:00 · S3 Bucket = Private · 11:00 · S3 Bucket = Public · 12:00 · S3 Bucket = Private
  • Config Timeline helps view: When do resources change · Configuration before/after · How compliance changes
  • Can link to CloudTrail to see which API call caused the change.

Config Rules

  • Rule is used to check configuration.
S3 Buckets
→ cannot be Public

Security Group
→ Do not open SSH all over the Internet

ALB
→ must have SSL Certificate
  • There are 2 types: AWS Managed Rule = Ready-made AWS
  • Custom Rule = your own logic = usually use Lambda

When does the rule run?

Can run:

Configuration Change
↓
Config evaluate immediately

or:

According to Schedule
↓
Config checked periodically
EC2 configuration changes
↓
Config Rule
↓
COMPLIANT / NON_COMPLIANT

Config DOES NOT block changes

Very important point:

User opens S3 Public
↓
Action happened first
↓
Config detected
↓
NON_COMPLIANT

Config detects after configuration occurs, not firewall to block previous action.

Notification

Khi resource NON_COMPLIANT:

AWS Config
↓
EventBridge / SNS
↓
Alert
Security Group opens port 22
↓
Config = NON_COMPLIANT
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS
↓
Security Team

  • Config can automatically edit resources through SSM Automation.
NON_COMPLIANT
↓

↓

↓
Fix configuration

↓

Remediation requires appropriate IAM permission.

Multi-Account / Multi-Region

AWS Config works by Region but can be aggregated:

Account A / Region A
Account B / Region B

        ↓

        ↓

  • Great for managing compliance for multiple AWS accounts.

S3 + Athena

  • Used for long-term audit.

  • Monitor application/server metrics · CPU / Logs / Errors

  ↓ Collect about

  ↓ Centralized Dashboard: Resource Inventory + Configuration History + Compliance Status
  • Config Aggregator only display synthesis and compliance testing; does not replace CloudTrail and does not automatically edit resources or create landing zones for new accounts.

Config detects a noncompliant bucket and a configured remediation restores the required setting.

5. Comparison

Config evaluates changes; preventive controls such as SCPs restrict actions before they occur.

CloudWatch vs CloudTrail vs Config

  • CloudWatch: How is the system running? Examples include high CPU, slow responses, or many failed requests.
  • CloudTrail: Who did what? Examples include changing a security group, deleting EC2, or calling an API.
  • AWS Config: How is a resource configured, and does it meet your rules? Examples include port 22 open to the Internet or a public S3 bucket.

Compare options

AWS X-Ray & Amazon Managed Grafana

1. Concept

X-Ray traces a request across services; Managed Grafana brings monitoring data into visual dashboards.

2. Purpose

Distributed trace of each request across a microservices architecture and visualize multi-source monitoring data on a unified dashboard.

3. How it works

  • Applications send traces to X-Ray; Grafana queries configured data sources to display dashboards.
  • Configure appropriate instrumentation, connectivity, and access permissions.

4. Practical example

A slow API trace identifies a costly database step, while related metrics appear in Grafana.

5. Comparison

Traces follow an individual request; metrics aggregate measurements; logs record detailed events.

AWS X-Ray & Amazon Managed Grafana Distinguishing Table

ServiceCore roleCentralized Integration & Configuration
AWS X-RayDistributed Tracing: records segments/subsegments and builds Service Map end-to-end to accurately localize services causing bottlenecks or 4xx/5xx errorsAPI Gateway, Lambda, ECS, EC2, SQS, SNS, DynamoDB, RDS integration; enabled via IaC (TracingConfig: Active on Lambda, TracingEnabled: true on API Gateway) with IAM permission to write trace
Amazon Managed GrafanaCompletely managed visual dashboard (no need to operate the Grafana server yourself)Concurrent queries from CloudWatch, Managed Prometheus, X-Ray, OpenSearch, Timestream, Athena, RDS; SSO login via IAM Identity Center

Summary

  • Amazon CloudWatch: Monitors system metrics and logs. Alarms notify or trigger actions when configured conditions are met.
  • Amazon EventBridge: Receives events and routes them to services using rules. For example, an order-cancellation event can trigger a refund workflow.
  • AWS CloudTrail: Records API activity to show who did what and when. Data operations such as reading S3 objects require the appropriate event configuration.
  • AWS Config: Tracks resource configurations and checks rules. Detecting noncompliance does not automatically prevent or fix changes without additional configuration.
  • X-Ray & Managed Grafana: X-Ray follows requests across services to locate slow steps. Grafana visualizes observability data from multiple sources on dashboards.

Security, Encryption & Threat Detection

AWS encryption patterns & KMS

1. Concept

Encryption makes data unreadable without a key; KMS manages keys and permission to use them.

2. Purpose

Understand the three places where encryption occurs and how AWS KMS manages encryption keys.

3. How it works

  • In Transit = encryption during transmission
  • At Rest = encryption at rest
  • Client-Side = the client encrypts itself before sending

Encryption in Transit

TLS / SSL
↓
HTTPS

Client
↓ encryption
Internet
↓
AWS Service
↓ decryption
Data
  • Protection from: Eavesdropping · Man-in-the-Middle
  • Certificates help clients authenticate endpoints.

Server-Side Encryption

  • The service automatically encrypts data before saving.
Client
↓ HTTPS
S3 / EBS / RDS
↓
Encrypt
↓
Ciphertext
↓
Storage

When reading:

Storage
↓
Decrypt
↓
Client
  • AWS services used KMS key/data key to perform encryption.

Client-Side Encryption

The client encrypts itself first:

Client
↓ Encrypt
Ciphertext
↓
S3 / DynamoDB / Storage
  • Storage only holds: Ciphertext
  • Only the client with the key can decrypt.

This is a stronger layer of protection if you want data to remain encrypted even for storage service administrators.

AWS KMS

  • KMS = manage encryption keys + control key usage + audit key usage
  • KMS integrates with: S3 · EBS · RDS · SSM · CloudTrail · IAM · ...
  • The application does not retrieve the plaintext KMS key.
Application
↓ KMS API
Encrypt/Decrypt
↓
KMS performs crypto operations

KMS Key Types

  • Symmetric = same key to Encrypt / Decrypt = commonly used AWS service
  • Asymmetric = Public Key + Private Key = Encrypt/Verify with public = Decrypt/Sign with private
  • Key types by ownership: AWS Owned Key = Fully managed by AWS
  • AWS Managed Key = AWS managed = alias of the form aws/service-name
  • Customer Managed Key = customer lifecycle management + policy = most detailed control

Customer Managed Key Suitable when you need custom key policy, cross-account, audit or lifecycle control.

Key Policy

  • Key policy: Defines who may manage or use a KMS key.
  • IAM policy: Does not guarantee key access by itself; the key policy must allow access directly or enable the account to delegate appropriate access through IAM.
  • Example: Role A needs permission to use kms:Decrypt on that key.
  • Cross-account access: Requires appropriate permissions in both the owner's key policy and the caller's IAM policy.
Key Policy
↓
Allow Role A
↓
kms:Decrypt

Rotation

AWS Managed Keys
→ AWS self-rotates

Customer Managed Key
→ automatic rotation can be enabled
  • Imported key material has different rotation limits.

KMS Key by Region

  • KMS key is Regional resource.
  • Region A · KMS Key A · Region B · KMS Key B

When copying encrypted snapshot to another Region:

Decrypt with Key A
↓
Encrypt with Key B
↓
Snapshot in Region B

Multi-Region Key

  • Multi-Region key: Related KMS keys in multiple Regions, with one primary key and replica keys.
  • Shared properties: They have the same key ID, key material, and rotation properties.
  • Example: An application encrypts data in Region A and uses the corresponding replica key to decrypt it in Region B.
  • Management: Each key is still managed in its own Region; this is not one global resource sharing every setting.

4. Practical example

An application stores records in S3 with SSE-KMS and grants decryption only to its processing role.

  • SSE-KMS for S3/EBS/RDS when needing CloudTrail audit, key policy and revoke access by key.
  • Client-side encryption for SSN/PII before replicating via DynamoDB Global Tables or Aurora Global Database.
  • Customer managed key and key policy have conditions to share encrypted snapshot/AMI for DR account.

5. Comparison

  • TLS protects data in transit;
  • encryption at rest protects stored data;
  • client-side encryption encrypts before uploading.

Compare 3 types of KMS Key, BYOK vs XKS & Default Encryption by Service

CriteriaAWS Owned KeyAWS Managed Key (alias/aws/...)Customer Managed Key (CMK)
Visible in KMS & CloudTrail?NoYesYes
Edit Key Policy / Cross-Account Sharing?NoNoYes (full control over rotation, policy, key deletion)
Locking costsFreeFree key maintenance (API fees may apply)Monthly key maintenance fee + API call fee
CriteriaBYOK (Import Key Material)XKS (External Key Store)
Key Material location of KMS KeyBorn outside import into AWS KMSAlways outside of AWS (on HSM on-premises), never import into AWS
Encryption Enforcement & Network DependencyAWS KMS processes itself, no need for constant on-prem connectionAWS KMS calls out to the external key system via XKS Proxy (loss of connection interrupts decryption)
Service groupList to remember when doing homework
Default encryption availableAmazon S3 (SSE-S3), DynamoDB (AWS Owned Key), SQS (SSE-SQS on new queue), Secrets Manager, CloudWatch Logs, Aurora (newly created cluster) and EFS created via Console
Must be enabled/configured upon creationEBS (unless EBS Encryption by Default is enabled in Region), Regular RDS, SNS Topic, Kinesis Data Streams, ElastiCache and EFS created via CLI/API
SSM Parameter Store & AWS Secrets Manager

1. Concept

Parameter Store and Secrets Manager store configuration or secrets such as passwords outside source code.

2. Purpose

Save configuration and secret outside source code.

3. How it works

  • Parameter Store = mainly saves configuration + can save simple secret = hierarchy/path
  • Secrets Manager = secret management = automatic rotation

SSM Parameter Store

  • Parameter Store is used to save: Configuration · Application settings · Simple secrets

For example:

  • /app/dev/db-host · /app/dev/db-password · /app/prod/db-host · /app/prod/db-password
  • Advantages: Serverless · Scalable · Durable · Version history · IAM access control

Application gets parameters via API:

Application/Lambda
↓
GetParameters
or
GetParametersByPath
↓
Parameter Store
↓
Value

Parameter Types

Secret can be saved as:

SecureString
↓
Encrypt using KMS
Application Role
↓ IAM allowed
Parameter Store
↓
KMS Decrypt
↓
Application receives plaintext value
  • → helps avoid hard-coding passwords/secrets in source code.

Parameter Hierarchy

Parameters can be organized by path:

  • /my-app/dev/db-password · /my-app/prod/db-password
  • Can be used: GetParametersByPath; to get parameters in a path.
/my-app/prod/
↓
db-host
db-user
db-password

Combining IAM:

Production Lambda
↓
read only
/my-app/prod/*
  • → easy to apply least privilege.

Parameter Store Integrations

  • Parameter Store integrates with: IAM = control permissions
  • KMS = SecureString encryption
  • EventBridge = notification/event
  • CloudFormation = use parameter in template

AWS also offers public parameters:

  • /aws/service/ami-amazon-linux-latest/...
  • → example used to get the latest Amazon Linux AMI ID.

You can reference secrets in Secrets Manager via:

  • /aws/reference/secretsmanager/...
Application
↓ GetParameter
Parameter Store
↓ reference
Secrets Manager
↓
Secret
  • → The real secret is still inside Secrets Manager, Parameter Store only provides reference for reading.

Parameter Tiers

  • There are 2 tiers: Standard · Advanced

AWS Secrets Manager

  • Secrets Manager specializes in: Passwords · Database credentials · API secrets
  • Secrets are: Encrypt using KMS
  • Strengths: Automatic Secret Rotation

AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)

ACM issues and manages TLS certificates for encrypted connections such as HTTPS on integrated AWS services.

Manage TLS certificates for HTTPS without having to handle the certificate lifecycle yourself.

  • → used for protection encryption in transit.

ACM Certificate

  • Certificate is attached at: TLS Termination

AWS CloudHSM

1. Concept

CloudHSM provides dedicated cryptographic hardware; you directly manage keys and HSM users.

2. Purpose

Use dedicated hardware HSM when customers need deeper control over keys, users, permissions and key material.

3. How it works

  • CloudHSM = dedicated Hardware Security Module = customer-specific hardware = customer key management + user + permission

HSM can be understood as:

"hardware safe"
↓
hold the cryptographic key
↓
Perform Encrypt/Decrypt/Sign
without giving out the plaintext key

What is CloudHSM?

  • CloudHSM: Dedicated hardware on AWS for protecting cryptographic keys. You control keys, HSM users, and permissions; AWS manages hardware and infrastructure.
  • Application access: Use the CloudHSM client/SDK to request encryption or signing; this is not a KMS API call by default.
  • Key protection: Cryptographic operations run inside the HSM; key material stays within the HSM in this usage pattern.
  • Capabilities: Supports encryption, signing, and cryptography such as AES, RSA, and ECC; hardware provides tamper resistance and FIPS compliance according to HSM type.
  • Cost: There is no free tier; use it when you need dedicated HSM control rather than only managing keys through KMS.

AWS WAF, Shield & Firewall Manager

1. Concept

WAF filters web requests, Shield mitigates DDoS, and Firewall Manager applies shared security policies across accounts.

2. Purpose

Protect Internet-facing workloads in 3 layers:

3. How it works

  • WAF = HTTP request filtering = Layer 7
  • Shield = DDoS protection
  • Firewall Manager = Centralized security policy management for multiple AWS accounts/resources

AWS WAF

  • AWS WAF protects applications in: Layer 7 = HTTP / HTTPS
  • WAF can be attached to: Application Load Balancer · API Gateway · CloudFront · AppSync · Cognito User Pool
  • Do not attach directly to: Network Load Balancer
  • because: NLB = Layer 4 = TCP / UDP
  • WAF = Layer 7 = HTTP

Web ACL

  • WAF uses: Web ACL = set of rules to decide Allow / Block request
HTTP Request
↓
WAF Web ACL
↓
Rules
↓
Allow / Block
  • Popular rules: IP Set = allow/block IP
  • According to the slides: IP Set · ≤ 10,000 IP addresses
  • String Match = check Header / Body / URI
  • Used to detect patterns such as: SQL Injection · XSS
  • Additionally: Size Constraint = check request size
  • Geo Match = filter by country
  • Rate-Based Rule = limit too many requests from one source

For example:

1 IP
↓ sending too many requests
Rate-Based Rule
↓
Block

Web ACL Scope

  • Web ACLs are typically: Regional
ALB

  • CloudFront specifically uses: CloudFront Scope

  • Rule Group = group multiple WAF rules = reusable
SQLi Rule
XSS Rule
Bad IP Rule

↓
Rule Group
↓
multiple Web ACLs
  • → helps reuse security rules.

AWS Shield

  • AWS Shield protects against: DDoS Attack
  • Especially in: Layer 3 / Layer 4
  • SYN Flood · UDP Flood · Reflection Attack

Shield Standard

  • Shield Standard = default enabled = free = baseline DDoS protection
  • AWS provides itself to customers.
  • Used to reduce common DDoS attacks at the network/transport layer.

Shield Advanced

  • Shield Advanced = Advanced DDoS protection
  • Protect resources such as: EC2 · ELB · CloudFront · Global Accelerator · Route 53
  • There are more: Advanced mitigation · 24/7 DDoS support · Cost protection
  • → suitable for workloads with high DDoS risk or requiring specialized support.
  • ≈ $3,000 / Organization / month

Pricing is subject to change. Check current AWS Pricing.

AWS Firewall Manager

  • Firewall Manager: Centrally manages security rules across AWS accounts in Organizations.
  • Scope: Select OUs or accounts; matching resources inside them receive the policy.
  • Example: Every public ALB in the Production OU must have the same WAF rules, including newly created ALBs.
  • Managed services: Can manage policies for WAF, Shield Advanced, security groups, Network Firewall, and Route 53 Resolver DNS Firewall.
  • Enforcement: Firewall Manager applies and checks policies; the underlying WAF, firewall, or security group filters traffic.

Details of AWS WAF Rate-Based Rule

  • WAF Rate-Based Rule Counts the number of requests from each source IP (or composite key) in a sliding window 5 minutes and automatically Block Temporarily exceed the IP threshold right at CloudFront, ALB or API Gateway without writing a Lambda to process logs.
